365 — Distributed Event Sourcing Database from Scratch

Project Number: 365

Project Name: Distributed Event Sourcing Database from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 Precise Engineering Objective

Build a distributed, append-only event store in the EventStoreDB lineage: per-stream immutable logs with optimistic concurrency (ExpectedVersion), a totally ordered global ($all) log addressed by GlobalPosition, aggregate replay via snapshot-plus-tail, idempotent checkpointed projections, persistent and volatile subscriptions, and Raft-based replication of the global log for quorum durability across a cluster.

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary In scope Out of scope
Storage Segment-file log, index, fsync, CRC, scavenge Cloud object storage backends
Concurrency Optimistic version check (ExpectedVersion) Pessimistic locks, two-phase commit
Replication Raft over global log; quorum commit Multi-master conflict resolution
Subscriptions Catch-up + live; persistent; competing consumers Fan-out push to external webhooks
Projections Continuous + one-time; rebuild; checkpoint Complex CEP / windowed aggregation
Partitioning Hash-based stream-to-node routing, rebalancing Cross-shard transactions
API surface gRPC + HTTP; cluster gossip SQL/GraphQL query layer
Event payload Opaque bytes; content-type tag; event-type versioning Schema registry; payload validation
Operations Online scavenge, backup/export pack Automatic cloud backups

1.3 Explicit Non-Goals

  • No in-place event mutation; corrections are new compensating events.
  • No cross-stream ACID transactions (single-stream atomicity only).
  • No built-in schema enforcement; callers own serialization.
  • No built-in authentication protocol (LDAP/OAuth); auth hook exists but provider is external.
  • No multi-region WAN replication; single-datacenter Raft groups assumed.
  • No SQL-based query planner over event data.
  • No live migration of segment format across running clusters.

1.4 Deployment Model

  • Cluster: 3 or 5 nodes, each running esdb-server; Raft group per partition.
  • Each node owns storage for its assigned partitions; followers replicate.
  • Single-node mode (no replication) supported for development and testing.
  • Clients connect via gRPC or HTTP; the server routes appends to the Raft leader.
  • Horizontal scaling via stream partitioning (Phase 12); capacity = add nodes + rebalance.

1.5 Target Users

User class Primary interaction
Application developer Append events; read streams; load aggregates; subscribe
Domain modeler Define aggregates + projections; use ExpectedVersion for concurrency
Platform/SRE operator Cluster lifecycle; scavenge scheduling; backup; monitoring
Tooling integrator esdb-cli; gRPC client generation; CDC tap via $all

1.6 Language Rationale: Rust (locked)

The core is an append-only log store (segment files, fsync, recovery) plus Raft replication plus projection processing — a storage + distributed-systems problem where the append/replay path must be allocation-light and the on-disk format must be corruption-resistant. Rust gives mmap/io control over segments, zero-copy event decode, exhaustive enums for the command/replication taxonomy, and no GC on the hot append and catch-up paths. The log, stream engine, projection engine, Raft replication, partition router, and server are all Rust; event payloads are opaque bytes (schema is the caller's concern, with versioning support).

All twelve crates are scaffolded in Phase 0; no crate is usable end-to-end until its owning phase lands.

1.9 Deferred Features

  • PhaseDeferred("§1.9") — D11 cross-partition $all merge (per-partition $all is Phase 12; merged global view is deferred past Phase 14; see Deferred Features Register).
  • PhaseDeferred("§1.9") — D12 OAuth/LDAP authentication provider (auth hook lands Phase 13; external provider integration is deferred; see Deferred Features Register).
  • PhaseDeferred("§1.9") — D13 backup streaming to S3-compatible object storage (see Deferred Features Register).
  • PhaseDeferred("§1.9") — D14 time-based event expiration TTL scavenge policy (maxcount scavenge is Phase 9; TTL-based is deferred; see Deferred Features Register).

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge Developed

Engineering muscle How this project exercises it
Append-only log storage Segment files, CRC integrity, torn-write recovery, index persistence — log as the canonical truth store
Optimistic concurrency at scale ExpectedVersion CAS without row locks; WrongExpectedVersion retry semantics; dedup by EventId
Total-order distributed log Mapping per-stream EventNumbers onto a monotonic GlobalPosition; replication preserves order
Raft consensus Leader append → quorum ack → commit; term-safe log truncation; follower catch-up
Projection and read-model architecture Idempotent handlers; checkpoint-resume; rebuild-from-zero as schema migration
Catch-up subscription boundary Position-safe handoff from historical replay to live tail without gap or duplicate
Stream partitioning Hash-routing; partition ownership; rebalancing without data loss
Online compaction (scavenge) Rewriting segments while live writers proceed; preserving position/number semantics
Snapshot-accelerated replay Fold-checkpoint semantics; invalidation on state schema change
Backpressure across async streams Subscription consumer that is slower than the log head; ack/nack/park/retry

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • EventStoreDB introduced the $all stream and commit/prepare position model, elevating an append log from a per-aggregate ledger to a global CDC bus — a design later echoed by Kafka, Kinesis, and Pulsar.
  • Greg Young's CQRS + Event Sourcing formalization (2010) established the aggregate-as-event-stream pattern; this project operationalizes all its moving parts end-to-end.
  • The combination of Raft-replicated log + MVCC-free optimistic concurrency is a cleaner model than 2PC-based event stores; it isolates consistency to a single ordered log.
  • Implementing projections on top of a checkpointed $all cursor is the foundational pattern for change data capture (CDC), materialized views, and read-model CQRS — all mainstream in production systems.
  • The scavenge (log compaction) design mirrors Kafka log compaction and RocksDB compaction; understanding it at this level clarifies the design of any log-structured storage engine.

2.3 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Manifestation in this project
Storage durability fsync policies; group commit; O_DIRECT bypass of page cache
Crash recovery Torn-tail truncation; WAL replay; index rebuild from log scan
Quorum durability Raft commit index = f+1 writes for f tolerated failures
At-least-once delivery → exactly-once effect Checkpoint + idempotent handler pattern
Linearizability All appends go through Raft leader; followers are read-only (with staleness caveat)
Observability Lag = GlobalPosition(head) − Checkpoint(subscription); latency histograms per operation
Backpressure Flow control in gRPC streaming; ack-window in persistent subscriptions
Online schema evolution Projection rebuild replaces migration; event-type versioning in EventRecord

2.4 Cross-Portfolio Links

Related project Shared concept
#296 — Event Sourcing Framework Aggregate + projection patterns; this project is the storage layer #296 would sit on
#69 — Write-Ahead Log Segment framing, CRC, torn-write recovery; esdb-log is a WAL specialization
#68 — LSM-Tree Compaction / scavenge patterns; index persistence; bloom filter analogues
#37 — Raft Consensus esdb-replication implements exactly the Raft spec; direct knowledge transfer
#103 — CDC (Change Data Capture) The $all stream is a CDC log; subscriptions are CDC consumers
#100 — MVCC + Transaction Manager Contrast: this project uses OCC (ExpectedVersion) not MVCC; highlights tradeoff

2.5 Engineering Capability Calibration

Completing Phases 1–9 (single-node store) reaches senior-level storage systems proficiency. Completing Phases 10–11 (Raft replication) requires principal-level distributed systems reasoning — log matching, safety under partition, liveness under timing. Completing Phases 12–14 (partitioning, production hardening) exercises system design at the staff/principal level: consistency vs. availability tradeoffs in sharded event stores, online compaction safety, conformance testing of distributed invariants.

2.6 What Mastery Looks Like

After completing this blueprint end-to-end, a practitioner commands the following concrete competencies:

  • Segment log engineering (Phases 2–4): can design a length-prefixed, CRC-checksummed, fsync-disciplined append-only log from first principles; can reason about torn-write detection, index rebuild, and group-commit trade-offs without consulting documentation.
  • Optimistic concurrency at the storage layer (Phase 3): can implement ExpectedVersion CAS semantics with per-stream striped locks, EventId deduplication, and gap-free numbering; can explain why MVCC is unnecessary and what pathologies it would introduce here.
  • Raft log replication and safety (Phases 10–11): can walk through the log-matching property, commit-index advancement, and leader-election safety proofs; can identify split-brain scenarios and implement CheckQuorum/PreVote defenses.
  • Projection and read-model architecture (Phase 8): can design idempotent, checkpointed handlers that achieve exactly-once effect from an at-least-once delivery substrate; can rebuild a projection schema without downtime using the disposable-read-model pattern (P9).
  • Catch-up/live subscription boundary (Phase 7): can implement the transition buffer / live-latch pattern that guarantees zero gaps and zero duplicates at the catch-up-to-live handoff — a subtle race that breaks most naive implementations.
  • Online scavenge / compaction safety (Phase 9): can design a segment-rewrite pipeline that preserves EventNumber and GlobalPosition semantics for surviving events, handles mid-scavenge crashes via atomic rename, and prevents scavenging events still in projections' lag windows.
  • Distributed stream partitioning (Phase 12): can implement consistent-hash routing, ownership transfer with lease fencing, and a cross-partition merge-sort cursor for $all; can articulate the trade-off between a global position arbiter and per-partition position spaces (ADR-23).

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Architecture Diagram

                       DISTRIBUTED EVENT SOURCING DATABASE  (append -> replicate -> project)
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |   clients (append / read / subscribe)                                               |
  |        |                                                                            |
  |        v                                                                            |
  |   esdb-server (gRPC/HTTP)  ---> esdb-partition (route by StreamId hash to owner)     |
  |        |                                |                                           |
  |        v                                v                                           |
  |   esdb-stream (append w/ ExpectedVersion; optimistic concurrency check)              |
  |        |  EventRecord                                                               |
  |        v                                                                            |
  |   esdb-replication (RAFT): leader appends to global log, replicates to followers     |
  |        |  commit when quorum-acked (commit_index)                                   |
  |        v                                                                            |
  |   esdb-log: PER-STREAM append-only log + GLOBAL ($all) log  (segments + index, fsync)|
  |        |                         |                          |                       |
  |        v                         v                          v                       |
  |   esdb-snapshot           esdb-subscribe (catch-up      esdb-storage (scavenge:      |
  |   (load = snap + tail)     + live, checkpointed)         compact deleted streams)    |
  |        |                         |                                                  |
  |        v                         v                                                  |
  |   aggregate state         esdb-projection (idempotent read models, checkpointed) --> |
  |                                  |                                  read models      |
  |                                  v                                                  |
  |                           esdb-query (read stream / $all / by category / projection) |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Responsibilities

clients (append / read / subscribe) External callers: application services appending domain events, query services reading streams or projections, workers consuming persistent subscriptions. Trust boundary: outside the process; all input is untrusted. Speak gRPC (primary) or HTTP/1.1 JSON (secondary). Deliver EventId for idempotency. Carry no cluster state; reconnect to the leader on redirect.

esdb-server (gRPC/HTTP) Entry-point process per cluster node. Accepts inbound RPCs; multiplexes Append, Read, Subscribe, Project, Scavenge, Cluster admin commands. Concurrency boundary: tokio runtime, one task per streaming RPC. Routes non-owner appends to the partition owner via esdb-partition. Owns connection lifecycle, TLS termination, auth hook, and health/gossip endpoints. Persistence boundary: none — delegates to lower crates.

esdb-partition (route by StreamId hash to owner) Hash StreamId to a partition slot; map slot to owning node via cluster membership table. Appends routed to the Raft leader of the owning group. Reads may be served locally from a follower with a staleness caveat. Rebalancing: ownership transfer triggers catch-up replication before serving. No storage; pure routing + membership state.

esdb-stream (append w/ ExpectedVersion; optimistic concurrency check) Per-stream state machine: current EventNumber head, tombstone flag, EventId dedup ring buffer. Enforces ExpectedVersion atomically before issuing an append to esdb-log. Assigns gap-free EventNumbers. Returns AppendResult{next_expected_version, commit_position}. Concurrency boundary: one write lock per stream (short-held); reads are lock-free on committed data. No direct disk I/O — delegates to esdb-log.

esdb-replication (RAFT) Implements Raft over the global log. Leader accepts ReplicationEntry from esdb-stream, writes to its local log, fans out AppendEntries RPCs to followers. Advances commit_index once a quorum acknowledges. Followers apply entries in order. Leader election: randomized election timeouts, term-safe vote grant, pre-vote optional. Persistence boundary: durable via esdb-log (the Raft log IS the global event log). Safety: only the leader appends; followers redirect clients.

esdb-log: PER-STREAM log + GLOBAL ($all) log (segments + index, fsync) Dual-log storage engine. Per-stream log: fixed-size segment files, length-prefixed + CRC records, sequential append, segment roll-over at configured size. Global ($all) log: same segment format, records ordered by GlobalPosition, written after stream append. Index: (stream, EventNumber) → file offset; GlobalPosition → file offset. fsync after each commit (or group-commit window). Recovery: scan last segment, detect torn tail by CRC failure, truncate, rebuild index from log scan. This is the only layer that writes to disk (aside from snapshots and projection state).

esdb-snapshot (load = snap + tail) Snapshot store keyed by (StreamId, EventNumber). Writes opaque Snapshot{stream, version, state, position} blobs written by the application at chosen intervals. Aggregate load: find newest snapshot with version <= target; replay tail events from that version. Invalidation: snapshot is invalidated when the application's state schema version changes (stored alongside the blob). Separate storage path from the event log; not subject to scavenge. Persistence boundary: own segment or flat file per stream in a snapshots/ directory.

esdb-subscribe (catch-up + live, checkpointed) Subscription engine. Catch-up phase: reads historical events from esdb-log from the subscriber's from position, delivers in order. Transition to live: at log head, switch to live tail feed without gap (position fence). Volatile subscriptions: in-memory only, lost on crash. Persistent subscriptions: checkpoint position durably; on reconnect, resume from checkpoint. Competing consumers: partition delivery across a consumer group; ack/nack/park/retry. Backpressure: pause delivery when consumer ack window is full.

esdb-storage (scavenge: compact deleted streams) Online scavenge: reads segments, builds a ScavengePlan of live ranges (events not soft-deleted, not over maxcount, not expired), rewrites segments retaining only live events, preserves original EventNumbers and GlobalPositions in rewritten records, swaps index. Safety: the scavenge plan is committed before rewriting; a crash mid-scavenge resumes from the plan. Checksum verification pass precedes any scavenge. Also owns index persistence: flushes in-memory index to disk and rebuilds from log on startup if index file is stale or missing.

esdb-projection (idempotent read models, checkpointed) Projection engine. Each projection is a named {query, checkpoint, state, mode}. Registered over $all or a filtered stream. Handler is called with each event; must be idempotent. State is serialized and persisted; checkpoint is GlobalPosition of last processed event. At-least-once delivery from esdb-subscribe + idempotent handler = exactly-once effect. Rebuild: reset checkpoint to 0, replay from start. OneTime mode: run once then seal. Continuous mode: run forever, track live tail.

esdb-query (read stream / $all / by category / projection) Read-path façade. ReadStream(stream, from, direction, count) → ReadResult. ReadAll(from, direction, count, filter) → ReadResult over $all. Category reads: filter by StreamId prefix. Event-type reads: filter by event_type field. Projection state reads: return last-checkpointed state blob. Consistent reads: read-your-writes guarantee on the leader; followers expose a min_position staleness parameter. Paging: next cursor + is_end flag.

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes
Append throughput (single node) ≥ 20,000 events/sec sustained Single writer, batch size 1–100; fsync amortized via group-commit
Append throughput (cluster, leader) ≥ 10,000 events/sec sustained Raft replication adds one RTT per quorum commit
Append p50 latency ≤ 1 ms Single node, local disk, batch=1
Append p99 latency ≤ 10 ms Includes fsync; SSD assumed
Append p99.9 latency ≤ 50 ms Tail includes segment roll-over + index flush
Replicated append p99 latency ≤ 25 ms Quorum of 3 nodes on LAN (< 1 ms RTT)
Stream read (catch-up) throughput ≥ 200,000 events/sec Sequential scan of segments; zero-copy decode
Stream read p50 latency (single event) ≤ 500 µs Index lookup + single read
Projection catch-up rate ≥ 100,000 events/sec Idempotent handler; no external I/O in handler
Subscription delivery lag at head ≤ 5 ms Live tail; event committed → delivered
Memory per idle stream ≤ 256 bytes Head version + dedup ring ref; streams are cheap
Memory per active subscription ≤ 64 KB Ack-window buffer + position state
Memory: global index (in-process) ≤ 1 GB for 10⁸ events ~10 bytes/entry for (position → offset) index
Memory: stream index (in-process) ≤ 500 MB for 10⁷ streams ~50 bytes/stream for head + segment pointer
Segment file size Configurable 64 MB – 1 GB Default 256 MB; roll-over when full
Max open file descriptors ≤ 1024 per node Active segments + index files; closed segments mmap'd
Storage amplification ≤ 1.05× for live data Scavenged data removed; CRC + length overhead < 5%
Startup time (cold, 10⁸ events) ≤ 30 s Index rebuild from log scan if index missing
Startup time (warm, index present) ≤ 5 s Load index file; verify last segment tail
Shutdown (graceful) ≤ 10 s Drain in-flight appends; flush index; fsync segments
Crash recovery time ≤ 60 s for 10⁸ events Tail-scan + truncation + index rebuild
Raft election timeout 150–300 ms randomized Standard Raft recommendation
Raft leader election latency ≤ 500 ms Time from leader loss to new leader serving appends
Max cluster size 5 nodes (3 or 5 recommended) Raft quorum; larger clusters reduce write throughput
Max partitions per cluster 256 Hash ring slots; rebalancing batch = 16 partitions
Max concurrent gRPC streams 10,000 per node tokio task per stream; 64-byte stack frame
Scavenge I/O impact ≤ 20% of append bandwidth Rate-limited compaction; configurable I/O budget
Event payload max size 4 MB Hard limit enforced at server; typical << 64 KB
Max events per stream Unlimited (u64 EventNumber) Scavenge + snapshot prevent unbounded replay cost
Max stream count 10⁸ per node Index memory constraint above
RTO (recovery time objective) ≤ 2 min (node failure, Raft cluster) Election + follower catch-up time
RPO (recovery point objective) 0 events (quorum-committed) Committed = quorum-durable; no committed event lost
Availability target 99.9% (3-node cluster, minority failure) Raft tolerates 1-of-3 failure
CPU architecture x86-64 (primary); aarch64 (secondary) CRC32 hardware instruction assumed
OS target Linux (primary); macOS (dev/test) io_uring optional; fallback to epoll/kqueue
Min disk IOPS 1,000 random IOPS For index lookups during catch-up reads
Recommended disk NVMe SSD, ≥ 500 MB/s sequential write Spinning disk supported with reduced throughput targets

4.2 Latency Budget Breakdown (Single Append, No Replication)

Step Budget
gRPC decode + auth check 50 µs
ExpectedVersion check (stream lock) 50 µs
EventNumber assignment + dedup check 20 µs
Log segment write (length-prefix + CRC) 200 µs
fsync (group-commit, 1 ms window) 500 µs
Index update (in-memory) 20 µs
AppendResult encoding + gRPC send 50 µs
Total p50 target ~900 µs

4.3 Replication Overhead (3-Node Raft, LAN)

Step Added latency
Leader → follower AppendEntries RPC +1 ms RTT
Follower disk write + ack +200–500 µs
Quorum ack → commit_index advance +200 µs
Total added p50 ~2 ms

4.4 Capacity Planning Reference

Metric Value
Event record wire overhead 64 bytes fixed header + payload
Index entry size (stream → offset) 16 bytes
Index entry size (position → offset) 12 bytes
Snapshot size Application-defined; recommend ≤ 1 MB
Checkpoint record size 32 bytes per subscription/projection
Segment header size 4 KB (reserved)
Target log read bandwidth for catch-up ≥ 500 MB/s (NVMe sequential)

B7 The Art of Immutability — Pat Helland (CIDR 2015 extended edition) Full text (~18 pp) Immutability as the foundation for scale and federation; append-only vs update-in-place; scavenging as explicit collection — reinforces P1/P2/P10 §6 P1 (Events Are Immutable), §6 P2 (Log Is Source of Truth), Phase 9 (scavenge as the only authorised removal path), ADR-09 (custom segment format rationale)
B8 Patterns of Enterprise Application Architecture — Martin Fowler Event Sourcing, CQRS, Identity Map, Repository chapters CQRS read/write model split; aggregate identity; repository as stream adapter; domain event vs system event distinction §3 (architectural overview — command vs query separation), §6 P9 (read models are disposable), Phase 8 (projection as CQRS read model), Phase 5 (esdb-query as read facade)
B9 Concurrency in Practice — Brian Goetz Ch. 13 (Explicit Locks), Ch. 15 (Atomic Variables + Nonblocking Algorithms) CAS-based optimistic concurrency patterns; lock-free queue design for subscription dispatch; relates to esdb-stream's version-check-then-append atomicity Phase 3.2 (StripedMutex + CAS version check), §6 P3 (Optimistic Concurrency), Phase 7.2 (bounded channel backpressure), §7 I5 (WrongExpectedVersion atomicity)
B10 Systems Performance — Brendan Gregg Ch. 8 (File Systems), Ch. 9 (Disks) fsync latency; buffered vs direct I/O; batch-commit coalescing; O_DIRECT considerations; page-cache interaction with segment reads; informs Phase 14 throughput tuning Phase 2.7 (batched fsync policy), Phase 4.7 (batch fsync group-commit), Phase 14.7 (fsync dominates p99, O_DIRECT for pack export), §4.2 (latency budget — fsync 500 µs)

6. Design Principles

P1 — Events Are Immutable and Append-Only

Statement. An EventRecord, once committed, has a fixed id, stream, number, position, data, and metadata. Correction is a new compensating event; deletion is a stream-level tombstone + deferred scavenge, not an in-place edit.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Corrective events add log volume; consumers must handle compensation. Gain: every observer can replay from any past position to reconstruct state.
Operational Scavenging (Phase 9) is the only authorised removal path; it operates offline on marked-deleted streams and preserves live events by rewriting segments.
Violation consequence Mutating a committed record invalidates all downstream projections, snapshots, and subscription checkpoints silently; Raft log-matching property breaks because followers hold the original bytes.
Enforcement esdb-log: segments are written once, never reopened for update; esdb-stream assigns EventNumber at append time and never reassigns.

P2 — The Log Is the Source of Truth

Statement. The durable state of the system is the event log (esdb-log). Aggregate state, read models, snapshots, and projections are derived views; they can always be discarded and rebuilt by replaying the log.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Cold rebuild from position 0 is slow for large logs — mitigated by snapshots (Phase 6) and projection checkpoints (Phase 8).
Operational Any corruption of a derived store (projection DB, snapshot store) is recovered by reset + replay, not point-in-time restore.
Violation consequence Treating a projection as authoritative and appending based on its state creates a split brain — the log may disagree with the projection after a rebuild.
Enforcement esdb-projection never writes to the log; esdb-snapshot is a cache layer, not a write-path.

P3 — Optimistic Concurrency on Append

Statement. Every append declares an ExpectedVersion; the stream engine checks the current stream tip atomically before committing. Mismatches produce WrongExpectedVersion with no write.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Contended streams (high-concurrency aggregate) see frequent rejections requiring client retry; serialise hot streams or shard aggregates to reduce contention.
Operational ExpectedVersion::Any bypasses the check for append-only streams (e.g. activity logs); use only when ordering doesn't matter.
Violation consequence Without the check, concurrent appenders overwrite each other's events; aggregate invariants are violated silently (double-booking, over-draw).
Enforcement esdb-stream: version check + append is a single critical section per stream; enforced before touching esdb-log.

P4 — Total Order in the Global Log

Statement. Every committed event receives a unique, strictly monotonic GlobalPosition. The $all stream is totally ordered by commit position; subscriptions, projections, and Raft replication all cursor on this single sequence.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff The global log is a serialisation bottleneck; throughput scales with single-leader append rate unless partitioned (Phase 12), at which point $all becomes a merge across partition positions.
Operational Subscription lag is head_position − checkpoint_position; both are GlobalPosition values, making lag unambiguous.
Violation consequence Two events with the same position create ambiguous $all ordering; projections produce non-deterministic results depending on tie-breaking.
Enforcement esdb-log global log allocates positions under a single writer lock (leader in Raft); position is assigned atomically with the segment append.

P5 — Idempotent, Checkpointed Projections

Statement. Projection handlers are pure functions of (current-state, EventRecord); the engine applies at-least-once delivery but achieves exactly-once effect because the handler is idempotent and the position is checkpointed durably before moving on.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Checkpoint write adds a durability sync per batch; tune checkpoint interval (every N events) to trade durability vs throughput.
Operational Rebuild = reset checkpoint to 0 + full replay from global log; no other migration needed (P9 — read models are disposable).
Violation consequence Non-idempotent handler + crash between apply and checkpoint = duplicate effect (double-billing, duplicate insert). Non-durable checkpoint + at-least-once = silent gap.
Enforcement esdb-projection: handler interface accepts (state, event) -> state; checkpoint stored in durable store alongside state; delivery is at-least-once but handler must be stateless or use position guard.

P6 — Replication Safety

Statement. An event is acknowledged to the client as committed only after a quorum of nodes has durably written it. A committed event is never lost to a minority failure and is never rolled back.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Quorum-sync append adds one network round-trip latency; pipeline multiple appends to hide it; read-your-writes requires routing to the leader or awaiting match_index.
Operational Minority partition causes leader step-down only after election timeout; inflight appends are retried, not lost, because uncommitted entries are pruned on leader change.
Violation consequence Acknowledging before quorum allows a node crash to lose the event; clients believe it committed, projections and aggregates diverge from the true log.
Enforcement esdb-replication: AppendResult returned only after commit_index advances past the entry's index; position emitted only post-quorum.

P7 — Per-Stream Version Monotonicity

Statement. EventNumbers within a stream are 0-based, gap-free, strictly increasing. No number is skipped on successful append; no number is reused after a tombstone.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Gap-free numbering requires atomic increment under the stream's critical section; concurrent appends serialise, which is acceptable because optimistic concurrency already serialises them.
Operational EventNumber is the address for Read(stream, from: EventNumber, count); a gap would make the read API non-deterministic.
Violation consequence A gap breaks aggregate snapshot validation (snapshot at version N, tail starts at N+2 — replay misses events N+1); concurrency check Exact(N) is meaningless if N is not the real tip.
Enforcement esdb-stream: tip stored in stream metadata; incremented atomically with segment write; never decremented.

P8 — Crash-Only Recovery

Statement. The system tolerates a kill-9 at any instant. Recovery truncates a torn tail from the last complete fsync boundary and rebuilds indexes from the log; no repair tooling or manual intervention needed.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff CRC per record adds ~4 bytes and one crc32c instruction per record; negligible overhead vs the safety guarantee.
Operational On startup, esdb-log scans the last segment from the last known-good offset, verifies CRCs, truncates at the first corrupt or incomplete record; then replays to rebuild in-memory index.
Violation consequence Serving a half-written record silently corrupts the stream's event sequence; clients receive corrupt data; EventNumber contiguity (I2) breaks.
Enforcement esdb-log: every record has a length-prefix + CRC32C; write-then-fsync before acknowledging append; truncate-on-open before serving reads.

P9 — Read Models Are Disposable

Statement. Any read model (projection state, snapshot, secondary index) can be dropped and rebuilt by replaying the log from GlobalPosition 0 (or the nearest valid checkpoint). Schema evolution of projections is a rebuild, not an in-place migration.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Rebuild time scales with log size; mitigated by partition-filtered replay (replay only the relevant category) and projection checkpoints.
Operational Deployment of a new projection version: increment projection schema version, drop old state, reset checkpoint = 0, let esdb-projection catch up.
Violation consequence Treating a read model as authoritative and migrating it in place risks data divergence if the migration contains a bug; a rebuild from the log is the only safe ground truth.
Enforcement esdb-projection exposes a rebuild(projection_name) API; esdb-snapshot exposes invalidate(stream, since_version).

P10 — Observability by Default

Statement. Every EventRecord carries its id, stream, number, and GlobalPosition. Subscription and projection lag is the scalar difference between the global head position and the last-checkpointed position, emitted as a gauge metric.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Per-event metadata overhead is fixed (≈40 bytes of structured fields per record); at high throughput this is dominated by the payload.
Operational lag = head_position − checkpoint.position; alert threshold configurable per subscription group; emitted via OpenTelemetry metrics.
Violation consequence Without position-embedded metadata, diagnosing a stuck projection requires log scraping; without lag metrics, SLO breaches are invisible until application breakage.
Enforcement esdb-core: EventRecord includes position field; esdb-subscribe + esdb-projection expose checkpoint position via metrics endpoint; esdb-server exports as OTel gauge.

P11 — Backpressure Is Applied at the Edge, Never on the Commit Path

Statement. A slow subscriber or projection handler is throttled at its own delivery channel; it never blocks, delays, or degrades the append critical path for other streams or other subscribers.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff A saturated subscriber channel means that specific consumer falls behind (bounded by channel capacity) rather than the whole server stalling; the tradeoff is per-consumer lag instead of global throughput loss.
Operational Each subscription has its own bounded channel (default sized to the 64 KB ack-window, §4.1); the log-tail publisher applies backpressure only to the lagging channel's producer task, never to esdb-log's append path.
Violation consequence Coupling append acknowledgment to subscriber drain rate turns one slow consumer into a cluster-wide outage; a single misbehaving projection handler could stall every writer.
Enforcement esdb-subscribe: bounded mpsc channel per subscription (Phase 7, ADR-03); esdb-log's commit path never awaits a subscriber; slow-consumer disconnection threshold configurable (Phase 13).

P12 — Partition Ownership Is Explicit and Redirect-Based, Never Silently Rerouted

Statement. Every StreamId maps to exactly one partition owner at any point in time via the consistent-hash ring; a node that is not the current owner rejects the write with a PartitionMoved redirect rather than silently forwarding or accepting it.

Aspect Detail
Tradeoff Explicit rejection-and-redirect adds one client round-trip on a stale routing table, versus transparent internal forwarding which would hide topology changes but risk writing to the wrong Raft group during a race.
Operational Routing table version is gossiped cluster-wide (Phase 12); clients cache the ring and refresh on PartitionMoved; rebalancing moves virtual nodes in small increments to bound unavailability.
Violation consequence Silent internal forwarding during a rebalance window can commit an event to the wrong partition's Raft group, breaking the per-partition position space and the category-affinity invariant.
Enforcement esdb-partition: PartitionRouter::route checks local ownership before accepting; mismatch returns PartitionMoved { correct_partition } (Phase 12, ADR-24).

7. System Invariants

Invariant Table

ID Statement Why It Matters Enforced In Violation Detection Telemetry Recovery Debug Path
I1 A committed EventRecord's id, stream, number, position, data, metadata never change Downstream replay produces the same aggregate state on every run; Raft log-matching relies on byte-identical entries esdb-log (segments are immutable once written); esdb-stream (no update path) CRC mismatch on re-read; hash-on-write vs hash-on-read mismatch logged as CORRUPTION esdb_corruption_total counter; segment + offset in structured log Halt reads from corrupt segment; recover from Raft follower or backup Compare CRC stored in index vs recomputed from bytes; check segment file modification timestamp
I2 EventNumbers in a stream are 0-based, gap-free, strictly increasing; no skip, no reuse Aggregate snapshot+tail reload depends on contiguous numbering; ExpectedVersion::Exact checks rely on exact tip esdb-stream: tip counter incremented atomically per append; tombstone marks stream closed but does not reuse numbers Read stream forward; assert events[i].number == start + i for all i; test in Phase 3 property suite esdb_stream_number_gap_total counter (should always be 0) Investigate stream metadata for correct tip; replay from 0 to find where gap originated Binary search stream log for missing number; inspect stream-metadata entry for tip vs log scan
I3 Commit positions are strictly increasing and unique across the entire store; $all order equals commit order Subscriptions, projections, and Raft all cursor on GlobalPosition; ambiguity creates non-deterministic projection output esdb-log global log position allocator (single writer, monotonic u64); esdb-replication enforces leader-only write Scan $all and assert pos[i+1] > pos[i]; duplicate detection in Phase 4 integration test esdb_position_monotonicity_violation_total (should always be 0) Identify the commit that produced the collision; trace to position allocator bug; halt all appends Dump global log index; compare sequential positions; identify which segment file contains duplicate
I4 An append of N events is all-or-nothing; no partial append is observable post-crash Aggregates assume a set of events was atomically committed together; partial commit leaves stream in inconsistent state esdb-log: write all N records then single fsync; esdb-stream: assign numbers and write segment atomically; Raft commit before acknowledging Recovery scan: count events in a transaction batch marker; if tail count < N, truncate esdb_partial_append_recovery_total (crash recovery counter) Truncate torn tail to last complete batch boundary on startup (P8) Inspect WAL tail for incomplete multi-record batch; correlate with AppendResult that was not returned to client
I5 An append whose ExpectedVersion != current stream tip is rejected with WrongExpectedVersion and writes nothing Prevents lost-update in concurrent aggregate writes; the concurrency contract aggregates rely on esdb-stream: version check + conditional write is a single critical section Integration test: concurrent appenders at same expected version — exactly one succeeds; others get WrongExpectedVersion esdb_wrong_expected_version_total per stream (normal ops metric; spike = contention) Client retries with fresh read of stream tip; no server-side recovery needed Log correlation: append request carries expected_version; stream metadata log carries current tip at rejection time
I6 Re-appending events with identical EventIds is a no-op; duplicates are not stored Network retry after a timeout must not produce duplicate events; EventId is the idempotency key esdb-stream: per-stream EventId dedup cache (last-N or bloom); esdb-replication: leader deduplicates before writing to global log Property test in Phase 3: append same EventIds twice, assert stream length unchanged esdb_idempotent_append_total counter (informational) No recovery needed; dedup is transparent Check if EventId appears in stream; correlate with AppendResult from the first successful commit
I7 An event reported committed is on a quorum; it survives any minority node failure; it is never rolled back Clients relying on committed events for downstream commands must not see those events disappear esdb-replication: AppendResult returned only after commit_index >= entry index; Raft safety theorem Raft linearizability test (Phase 14): kill minority after commit; verify event still readable on surviving quorum esdb_commit_quorum_ack_latency_ms histogram; esdb_uncommitted_entry_rollback_total (must be 0) Leader election brings a node with the committed entry; Raft log reconciliation removes uncommitted tail Compare commit_index vs match_index per follower; identify which node missed the entry
I8 Replaying a projection from position 0 through the same events yields the same state as the incremental live state Rebuild correctness; P9 (read models are disposable) depends on deterministic replay esdb-projection: handler is a pure (state, event) -> state function; no hidden mutable side effects allowed Phase 14 replay-equivalence test: compare snapshot of incremental state vs cold rebuild state byte-for-byte esdb_projection_replay_divergence_total (must be 0 in regression suite) If divergent, handler has a side effect or clock dependency; fix handler; rebuild from 0 Diff incremental state vs rebuilt state at the diverging position; inspect handler for timestamps, random, or external calls
I9 Loading via newest-snapshot-plus-tail equals folding the full stream from EventNumber 0 Snapshot load must be transparent; aggregate behaviour must not depend on whether a snapshot was used esdb-snapshot: snapshot stores full fold state at a specific EventNumber; tail replay starts at snapshot.version + 1 Property test (Phase 6): random stream, random snapshot frequency; assert fold-all == snap+tail for all positions esdb_snapshot_fidelity_mismatch_total (must be 0) Invalidate snapshot for the stream; reload by full fold Compare fold-all state vs snap+tail state at the same target version; inspect snapshot serialisation for lossy encoding
I10 Scavenging preserves all live events with their original EventNumbers and GlobalPositions; only deleted/expired/over-maxcount events are removed Consumers holding a position cursor must not encounter shifted positions post-scavenge esdb-storage: scavenge rewrites segments retaining live events at their original numbers and positions; does not compact positions Post-scavenge verification: scan original position list for live events; assert all still readable at same position esdb_scavenge_live_events_removed_total (must be 0); esdb_scavenge_segments_rewritten_total Halt scavenge job; restore segment from backup; re-run scavenge with dry-run mode to inspect ScavengePlan Diff pre/post scavenge position index; identify any live event whose position is absent post-scavenge
I11 Every StreamId is owned by exactly one partition at any point in time; a node that is not the current owner never accepts or forwards a write for it Concurrent acceptance by two nodes for the same stream would split a single logical Raft group's log into two, breaking per-stream version monotonicity (I2) across partitions esdb-partition: PartitionRouter::route checks the local ring's ownership entry before handing off to esdb-stream; mismatch is rejected, never forwarded Property test (Phase 12): 100k random StreamIds probed against every node; assert exactly one node accepts each esdb_partition_ownership_conflict_total (must be 0) Refresh routing table from gossip; if two nodes both believe they own the same partition, the higher ring-epoch wins and the other steps down Compare each node's local ring epoch and ownership table for the disputed partition; inspect gossip log for the last ring update
I12 A subscriber's or projection's channel saturation never blocks, delays, or degrades the append critical path for any other stream or subscriber Coupling commit acknowledgment to consumer drain rate would let one slow reader stall all writers cluster-wide, defeating the durability/availability targets in §4.1 esdb-subscribe: per-subscription bounded channel; esdb-log's commit path never awaits a subscriber task (P11) Load test (Phase 7/13): one subscriber paused indefinitely; assert append p99 for unrelated streams stays within §4.1 budget esdb_append_latency_ms histogram partitioned by presence of a stalled subscriber (must show no regression) Disconnect or park the stalled subscriber past its lag threshold; other subscriptions unaffected Inspect per-subscription channel depth metric; identify the channel at capacity and its owning subscription id

8. Domain Model

8.1 Entity Taxonomy

Entity Crate Key Mutable? Notes
StreamId esdb-core string (e.g. "order-1234") No category = prefix before first '-'
EventId esdb-core Uuid v4/v7 No idempotency key; globally unique
EventNumber esdb-core u64, zero-based No per-stream, gap-free
GlobalPosition esdb-core u64, monotonic No commit position in $all log
EventRecord esdb-event (stream, number, position) No immutable once committed
Snapshot esdb-snapshot (stream, version) Replaced newer snapshot supersedes older
Segment esdb-log (stream global, base_offset) Append-only
Checkpoint esdb-projection / esdb-subscribe subscription or projection id Updated advancing; never decreasing
ReplicationEntry esdb-replication (term, position) No Raft log entry
ScavengePlan esdb-storage scavenge_id Created once describes live-range bitmap per segment

8.2 Event Record Layout (wire + storage)

EventRecord on-disk frame (length-prefixed, inside segment):
  [frame_len: u32 LE]
  [crc32c: u32]
  [version: u8]          # envelope version
  [flags: u8]            # 0x01 = tombstone, 0x02 = snapshot
  [event_id: 16 bytes]   # UUID bytes
  [stream_len: u16 LE]   # byte length of stream id
  [stream: variable]
  [number: u64 LE]       # EventNumber
  [position: u64 LE]     # GlobalPosition (0 = not-yet-assigned in WAL)
  [event_type_len: u16 LE]
  [event_type: variable] # UTF-8 string
  [content_type: u8]     # 0=binary, 1=json, 2=avro, 3=proto
  [created_ns: u64 LE]   # Unix nanos
  [metadata_len: u32 LE]
  [metadata: variable]   # Bytes (opaque; structured e.g. causation-id, correlation-id)
  [data_len: u32 LE]
  [data: variable]       # Bytes (opaque payload)

Alignment: none required; CRC covers bytes from version through end of data. Torn write detection: frame_len read first; if segment tail < frame_len, truncate.

8.3 Index Layout

Two independent index structures per log (stream-log and global-log):

Stream index: (StreamId, EventNumber) -> (segment_id: u32, byte_offset: u64)
Global index: GlobalPosition          -> (segment_id: u32, byte_offset: u64)
Category index: category (StreamId prefix) -> sorted list of GlobalPositions
EventType index: event_type string -> sorted list of GlobalPositions

On-disk index file per segment: sorted array of fixed-size index entries.

IndexEntry (stream): [stream_hash: u64][number: u64][segment: u32][offset: u64] = 28 bytes
IndexEntry (global): [position: u64][segment: u32][offset: u64] = 16 bytes

Index rebuilt from segment scan on crash recovery (P8).

8.4 Stream State Machine

States: NonExistent | Active | Deleted (soft) | Tombstoned (hard)

Transitions:
  NonExistent  --[append, ExpectedVersion::NoStream]--> Active
  NonExistent  --[append, ExpectedVersion::Any]--> Active
  NonExistent  --[append, ExpectedVersion::Exact(n)]--> Error(WrongExpectedVersion) if n != 0
  Active       --[append, ExpectedVersion::Exact(n)]--> Active  (n == current_version)
  Active       --[append, ExpectedVersion::Exact(n)]--> Error(WrongExpectedVersion) if n != current_version
  Active       --[soft-delete cmd]--> Deleted
  Active       --[tombstone cmd]--> Tombstoned
  Deleted      --[scavenge]--> events removed, metadata retained
  Deleted      --[append]--> Error(StreamDeleted)
  Tombstoned   --[append]--> Error(StreamDeleted)
  Tombstoned   --[read]--> Error(StreamDeleted)

8.5 Optimistic Concurrency — ExpectedVersion Semantics

ExpectedVersion Accept condition Reject condition
Any always never
NoStream stream state == NonExistent stream exists (any events present)
StreamExists stream state == Active stream does not exist
Exact(n) current last EventNumber == n any other last version

Atomicity: version check and EventNumber assignment occur in a single write-lock scope within esdb-stream; no observer can see a partial batch.

8.6 Global Log and Commit Position

Prepare position: byte offset where the event was staged in the stream WAL.
Commit position:  GlobalPosition assigned when all events in a transaction reach quorum.

$all log ordering invariant (I3):
  for any two commits C1 < C2: C1.position < C2.position and C1 is visible before C2.

Position 0 is reserved; first committed event has position >= 1.

Position delta used as lag metric: head_position - checkpoint.position (P10).

8.7 Replication Log Entry

ReplicationEntry layout:
  term: u64          # Raft term when entry was created by leader
  position: u64      # GlobalPosition of this event
  stream: StreamId
  number: EventNumber
  payload_ref: u64   # byte offset in the per-stream log (avoids double-storing)

Leader appends to esdb-log, then replicates entry to followers. Follower applies by writing the entry locally. Commit index advances when match_index[i] >= entry.index for a quorum of nodes.

8.8 Snapshot State Machine

States: None | Valid | Stale (schema_version mismatch)

Transitions:
  None  --[periodic checkpoint]--> Valid(version=N, state=Bytes)
  Valid --[new snapshot at version M > N]--> superseded (retain M only)
  Valid --[schema_version bump in projection]--> Stale
  Stale --[aggregate load]--> ignored; fallback to full replay from 0

Load algorithm:

  1. Fetch newest Snapshot where version <= target_version and schema_version == current.
  2. If found: deserialize state; replay tail events [snapshot.version+1 .. target].
  3. If not found: replay from EventNumber 0.

Invariant I9: result is identical to full replay.

8.9 Subscription State Machine

States: Created | CatchingUp | Live | Paused | Terminated

Transitions:
  Created    --[subscribe(from_position)]--> CatchingUp
  CatchingUp --[reached log head]--> Live (atomic with last batch delivery)
  Live       --[new committed event]--> Live (push event to consumer)
  Live       --[consumer backpressure]--> Paused
  Paused     --[consumer ready]--> Live or CatchingUp (if gap accumulated)
  Live       --[unsubscribe / error]--> Terminated
  CatchingUp --[unsubscribe / error]--> Terminated

Catch-up/live boundary: read up to head_position in CatchingUp; atomically register live listener; flush any events that arrived during final read; transition to Live with no gap and no duplicate.

8.10 Projection State Machine

States: Idle | Running | Faulted | Stopped | Rebuilding

Transitions:
  Idle      --[start]--> Running
  Running   --[handler panic / serialization failure]--> Faulted
  Faulted   --[admin restart]--> Running
  Running   --[admin stop]--> Stopped
  Stopped   --[admin start]--> Running
  Running   --[admin rebuild]--> Rebuilding (reset checkpoint to 0)
  Rebuilding --[replayed to head]--> Running

Checkpoint write: after each batch of events; durably before acknowledging. Idempotency: handler applied to the same event at the same checkpoint position yields the same read-model mutation (I8).

8.11 Scavenge Plan and Compaction

ScavengePlan:
  scavenge_id: Uuid
  segments: Vec<(segment_id, live_bitmap: RoaringBitmap)>
  initiated_at: u64  # unix nanos
  status: Planned | Running | Complete | Aborted

Live-bitmap maps record slot indices within a segment to keep/drop. Scavenge rewrites each segment in-place to a new file with only live records; positions and EventNumbers of surviving events are preserved (I10). Deleted stream marker kept; $all positions remain monotonic (gaps become tombstone-markers in $all reads).

8.12 Partition Routing Model

partition_id = fnv1a64(stream_id.bytes) % num_partitions
owner_node   = partition_table[partition_id]

Routing table broadcast via cluster gossip. Write path: client -> any node -> route to owner -> append locally -> Raft replication within partition group. $all across partitions: merge-sort by GlobalPosition using per-partition cursors (not guaranteed globally contiguous; partitions have independent position spaces unless using a global position arbiter — ADR-23).

8.13 Worked Example — Happy-Path Append

Client:  Append("order-1234", ExpectedVersion::Exact(2), [e3, e4])
  -> esdb-server: route to owner node for "order-1234"
  -> esdb-stream: acquire write lock on "order-1234"
     read current last EventNumber = 2  (matches Exact(2))
     assign e3.number=3, e4.number=4
     write to per-stream segment: [frame(e3), frame(e4)] + fsync
  -> esdb-log: assign GlobalPosition 901 to e3, 902 to e4
  -> esdb-replication: create ReplicationEntry{term=7, position=901, ...}, {902, ...}
     replicate to 2/3 followers; advance commit_index to 902
  -> AppendResult{next_expected_version: EventNumber(4), commit_position: GlobalPosition(902)}

8.14 Failure-Path Example — Optimistic Concurrency Conflict

Concurrent writers W1 and W2 both read last version = 5:
  W1: Append("acct-99", ExpectedVersion::Exact(5), [debit])  -> accepted, assigns version 6
  W2: Append("acct-99", ExpectedVersion::Exact(5), [credit]) -> rejected
      Error: WrongExpectedVersion { stream: "acct-99", expected: Exact(5), actual: 6 }
W2 must re-read stream, reconcile, retry with ExpectedVersion::Exact(6).
No partial write from W2 is observable.  Invariant I5 holds.

8.15 Edge Cases and Invariant Stress Points

Edge Case Invariant Handling
Re-append same EventId I6 dedup map (stream -> Set<EventId>); return original AppendResult
Append 0 events I4 no-op; return current AppendResult immediately
ExpectedVersion::NoStream on existing stream I5 WrongExpectedVersion
Torn segment tail on crash P8/I4 truncate to last complete + CRC-verified frame
Follower ahead of leader (stale leader) I7 leader rejects its own appends when it sees higher term
Snapshot schema_version mismatch I9 Snapshot marked Stale; full replay forced
Scavenge on active stream I10 scavenge skips live tombstone events; positions preserved
Catch-up/live race I3 final catch-up batch under log-head latch; no duplicate delivery
EventNumber gap after crash I2 replay segment from base; reject writes with non-contiguous number
Projection handler panics I8 transaction rolled back; checkpoint not advanced; retry
Partition rebalance mid-append — old owner rejects after ownership transferred; client retries
$all read crossing partition boundary I3 merge-sort cursor; position monotonicity guaranteed per-partition
Competing consumers double-deliver — ack required before removal from pending; nack -> requeue
Stream tombstone with pending snapshot I10 snapshot deleted; tombstone written to $all; scavenge clears data frames

8.16 EBNF — Query Grammar (read API)

read_target  ::= "$all" | stream_id | "$category-" category | "$et-" event_type
stream_id    ::= category "-" local_id
category     ::= [a-zA-Z][a-zA-Z0-9_]*
local_id     ::= [a-zA-Z0-9_-]+
event_type   ::= [a-zA-Z][a-zA-Z0-9_.]*
from_pos     ::= "start" | "end" | position_literal
direction    ::= "forward" | "backward"
count        ::= [1-9][0-9]*           # max 4096 per page
read_req     ::= read_target from_pos direction count

8.17 Serialization Framing EBNF

frame       ::= frame_len crc32c version flags event_id stream_field number position
                event_type_field content_type created_ns metadata_field data_field
frame_len   ::= uint32_le   (* total bytes from version to end of data_field *)
crc32c      ::= uint32_le   (* over bytes [version .. data_field end] *)
string_field::= uint16_le body   (* body: UTF-8 bytes *)
bytes_field ::= uint32_le body

8.18 Raft State Transition (condensed)

Role transitions:
  Follower  --[election timeout, no heartbeat]--> Candidate
  Candidate --[votes received from quorum]--> Leader
  Candidate --[higher term seen]--> Follower
  Candidate --[election timeout again]--> Candidate (new term)
  Leader    --[higher term seen]--> Follower
  Leader    --[quorum unreachable]--> stays Leader until step-down timeout

Leader invariants:
  - AppendEntries sent every heartbeat_interval (< election_timeout_min)
  - Only appends with term == current_term
  - commit_index advances only when match_index[quorum] >= entry_index

8.19 Competing Consumers Protocol

Persistent subscription group G has N consumers C1..CN:
  Available event delivered to exactly one consumer (round-robin or least-pending).
  Consumer must Ack(event_id) within ack_timeout.
  On Nack(event_id, action=Retry): event re-queued after nack_sleep_ms.
  On Nack(event_id, action=Park):  event moved to parked queue; no retry.
  On timeout:                      event treated as Nack(Retry); retry_count++.
  On retry_count > max_retries:    auto-park.

Group checkpoint = min(acknowledged_position across all consumers).
Parked queue accessible via admin API; replay on demand.

State tracked per in-flight event:

Field Type Notes
event_id Uuid correlates Ack/Nack to delivery
position GlobalPosition for checkpoint advancement
consumer_id NodeId which consumer holds it
delivered_at u64 nanos timeout base
retry_count u16 park threshold

8.20 Backpressure Model

Layer 1 (transport): gRPC server-side streaming flow control; H2 window.
Layer 2 (subscription channel): bounded async channel (capacity = high_water_mark).
  Producer (catch-up reader or live pusher) blocks when channel full.
Layer 3 (consumer ack window): max_in_flight events; producer pauses at limit.

Metrics:
  subscription_lag_events: head_position - checkpoint.position
  subscription_channel_fill_ratio: len / capacity
  consumer_ack_pending: current in-flight count

Slow consumer effect: catch-up reader slows I/O read rate proportionally; live events queue in bounded channel; producer signals Paused to log reader.

8.21 Event Type Versioning

Strategy Mechanism Invariant
Up-cast projection handler maps old event_type to new shape I8: same replay result
New type new event_type emitted alongside old; handler handles both I1: old records unchanged
Schema hash in metadata schema_version key in metadata Bytes (JSON or custom) snapshot stale detection (§8.8)
Content-type negotiation content_type byte in frame (json/avro/proto) reader selects deserializer

Projection rebuild triggered on schema_version bump: reset checkpoint to 0, replay from beginning (P9, I8).

8.22 Category Stream Semantics

$category-<C> is a virtual stream containing all events from streams where
  category(stream_id) == C.
  category("order-1234") = "order"
  category("order")      = "order"   (no '-' -> whole name is category)
  category("acct-99-sub") = "acct"   (first '-' is the delimiter)

$all is a virtual stream: all events across all streams in commit order.
$et-<T> is a virtual stream: all events with event_type == T in commit order.

Virtual streams are served by index lookup (category/event-type secondary indexes),
not by storing a separate log. Phase 5 Read API builds these with server-side filter.

8.23 Sequence Diagram — Catch-up Subscription to Live

Client            esdb-server          esdb-subscribe        esdb-log
  |                    |                     |                    |
  |--Subscribe(from=P)-|                     |                    |
  |                    |--create sub, state=CatchingUp----------->|
  |                    |                     |                    |
  |                    |<-batch(P..P+99)-----|<--read forward(P)--|
  |<--events(P..P+99)--|                     |                    |
  |                    |...(repeat batches)..|                    |
  |                    |                     |--register live latch|
  |                    |<-batch(Ptail-9..Ptail)---[last catch-up]--|
  |<--events(..Ptail)--|                     |                    |
  |                    |                state=Live                |
  |                    |<-new event(Ptail+1)-|<--push on commit---|
  |<--event(Ptail+1)---|                     |                    |

No gap: live latch registered before final catch-up batch is delivered. No duplicate: position cursor is exclusive on next batch start.

8.24 Raft Append Sequence Diagram

Leader(L)          Follower(F1)       Follower(F2)
  |                     |                  |
  |--AppendEntries(entry{term,pos,record})->|
  |--AppendEntries(entry{term,pos,record})------------>|
  |<--AppendEntries_Resp(success, match_index=N)-------|
  |<--AppendEntries_Resp(success, match_index=N)--|
  | (quorum=2/3 matched; advance commit_index to N)
  |--AppendEntries(commit_index=N)----------->|
  |--AppendEntries(commit_index=N)--------------------->|
  | (both followers apply committed entries to local log)
  | AppendResult{commit_position=N} returned to client

8.25 Position Semantics — Prepare vs Commit

Position type When assigned Durability Used by
Prepare position when event enters stream WAL buffer NOT durable alone internal staging only
Commit position (GlobalPosition) when quorum ack received quorum-durable (I7) subscriptions, projections, replication cursor, $all reads

Clients only receive GlobalPosition in AppendResult. Subscriptions and projections cursor only on committed positions. Prepare positions are internal to the log write path.

8.26 Storage Layout on Disk

data/
  streams/
    <stream_hash_prefix>/
      <stream_id>/
        0000000000.seg      # segment file; base offset in name
        0000000000.idx      # stream index for this segment
        0001000000.seg
        0001000000.idx
  global/
    0000000000.seg          # $all log segments
    0000000000.idx          # GlobalPosition -> (segment, offset)
  snapshots/
    <stream_id>/<version>.snap
  projections/
    <projection_name>/
      state.db              # sled or RocksDB
      checkpoint            # single 8-byte GlobalPosition
  subscriptions/
    <sub_id>/
      checkpoint            # 8-byte GlobalPosition
  cluster/
    raft_log/               # ReplicationEntry segments
    raft_state              # current_term, voted_for
    membership.json         # cluster node list

8.27 Failure Mode Interaction Matrix

Failure Invariant at Risk Detection Recovery
Torn segment write I4, I2 CRC mismatch or frame_len overrun at tail truncate to last valid frame; rebuild index from base
Index file corruption I2, I3 checksum on index file header delete index file; rebuild from segment scan on startup
Leader crash mid-replicate I7 quorum absent; new election new leader has all committed entries; uncommitted entries discarded
Double-append same EventId I6 EventId dedup map in esdb-stream return original AppendResult; no new frame written
Snapshot deserialization failure I9 serde error on load mark snapshot Stale; fall back to full replay
Projection handler panic I8 std::panic::catch_unwind log error; do not advance checkpoint; retry after backoff
Scavenge deletes live event I10 live-bitmap computed before segment rewrite pre-check: bitmap must include all events not in delete set; abort on mismatch
Clock skew on created_ns I1, I3 monotonic global position never uses wall clock for ordering position is the ordering key; wall clock is metadata only
Network partition (split-brain) I7 minority nodes cannot reach quorum Raft prevents minority leader from committing; clients receive error

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
ADR-01 Implementation language Rust (stable toolchain, MSRV pinned) Go, C++, Java Memory safety without GC; zero-cost abstractions; Tokio async; rich ecosystem for low-level I/O Go wins when team velocity matters more than allocation control and GC pauses are acceptable (< 50k events/s); C++ wins in embedded/kernel contexts where no runtime is allowed; Java/GraalVM wins when JVM ecosystem integrations dominate.
ADR-02 Build system Cargo workspace (single Cargo.toml at root) Bazel, Buck Standard for Rust; monorepo support; no extra toolchain Bazel wins when the repo spans multiple languages (Rust + Go + Java) in a single monorepo requiring hermetic cross-language builds and remote caching at Google/Meta scale.
ADR-03 Async runtime Tokio (multi-thread scheduler) async-std, smol, single-thread Mature, production-proven; tokio::fs for async I/O; wide ecosystem compatibility Single-threaded runtime wins for latency-sensitive embedded systems where deterministic scheduling and zero cross-thread migration overhead outweigh parallelism; smol wins when binary size is paramount (IoT targets).
ADR-04 gRPC framework tonic + prost grpc-rs (C bindings), tarpc Pure Rust; code-gen from proto3; streaming support; H2 multiplexing grpc-rs (C bindings to grpc-core) wins when conformance with the official gRPC C-core interceptors and proxy integrations (Envoy xDS) is required; tarpc wins for internal trusted-boundary RPC where schema evolution is unnecessary.
ADR-05 HTTP/REST layer axum (atop hyper) actix-web, warp Composable middleware; shares Tokio runtime; low overhead actix-web wins at extreme HTTP/1.1 throughput benchmarks (TechEmpower) where its actor model and per-core thread affinity outperform axum's composable extractors; warp wins for tiny codebases preferring a filter-combinator DSL over macros.
ADR-06 Serialization — event payload framing Custom length-prefix + CRC32C (esdb-log) Cap'n Proto, FlatBuffers for WAL Direct control over binary layout; no schema dependency in hot path; CRC in frame Cap'n Proto wins when zero-copy in-place parsing of structured events (without a CRC scan) is the primary concern and a code-gen schema is acceptable in the hot path; FlatBuffers wins for shared-memory IPC where mmap is the transport.
ADR-07 Serialization — RPC wire format Protocol Buffers 3 (prost) JSON, MessagePack, FlatBuffers Compact; schema-versioned; field evolution; tonic native JSON wins when human-readable wire traffic is a hard requirement (debug-ability over a telnet/curl session without tooling); MessagePack wins for schema-less event stores where no code-gen step is tolerable and payload size matters more than field evolution.
ADR-08 Serialization — internal config/state TOML (config files), JSON (projection state) YAML, CBOR TOML: human-editable; JSON: projection state is user-facing YAML wins when the config DSL must support multi-document files, anchors, and inheritance (e.g., Kubernetes-style cluster config with complex override layers); CBOR wins when projection state is binary-heavy (embedding image bytes) and JSON base64 overhead is unacceptable.
ADR-09 On-disk segment format Custom fixed-structure segments (esdb-log) RocksDB, LMDB, SQLite Append-only by construction; no external lock manager; full control over fsync and CRC RocksDB wins when the workload is mixed read/write with frequent point lookups and range scans across arbitrary keys (not sequential event numbers); LMDB wins for read-heavy workloads where copy-on-write B-tree with MVCC provides serializable reads without a write lock.
ADR-10 Index persistence Custom sorted binary index files (per segment) BTreeMap serialized, SQLite Co-located with segment; rebuild-from-scan on crash; no separate index daemon SQLite wins when the index must support ad-hoc SQL queries over event metadata (e.g., SELECT * FROM events WHERE event_type = 'X' AND created > T) that would require custom secondary-index logic otherwise.
ADR-11 Consensus / replication Raft (from-scratch in esdb-replication, using raft-rs or openraft crate) Paxos, Viewstamped Replication, Zab Understandable; strong safety proofs; log matching maps directly to esdb-log Paxos (Multi-Paxos) wins in systems requiring decoupled leader election and log entry assignment (e.g., Chubby, Spanner) where flexibility in phase ordering matters; Zab wins when total-order broadcast with primary-backup semantics (ZooKeeper's model) is more natural than Raft's log abstraction.
ADR-12 Raft library dependency openraft (optional dependency) or bespoke thin layer raft-rs (etcd port), ratis openraft actively maintained, pluggable storage; bespoke keeps binary lean raft-rs wins when exact etcd behavioral compatibility (joint consensus wire format, pre-vote protocol version) is required for cross-cluster federation; ratis (Java) wins when the rest of the system is JVM-based and sharing an event loop with Netty is the goal.
ADR-13 Stream concurrency control Per-stream RwLock + version compare in esdb-stream MVCC table, global lock, CRDT Optimistic concurrency per stream; no cross-stream contention; aligns with P3 MVCC wins when concurrent reads vastly outnumber writes and readers must never block on writers (e.g., analytics workloads scanning millions of versions simultaneously); CRDTs win for geo-distributed eventual-consistency scenarios where merge semantics replace conflict rejection.
ADR-14 Snapshot storage Separate key-value store (sled or custom B-tree file) keyed by (stream_id, version) Same segment log, SQLite Snapshots are large blobs; random access by key; separate from append-only log Storing snapshots in the same segment log wins when the snapshot store must be replicated via Raft (log-structured replication) and isolation from the event log is operationally complex; SQLite wins when snapshot metadata queries (list by schema_version, size) outweigh the simplicity of flat files.
ADR-15 Projection state storage sled or RocksDB per projection (pluggable backend) In-memory only, PostgreSQL Pluggable; projection state is disposable (P9); sled is pure Rust In-memory wins for ephemeral projections that are always rebuilt from scratch on restart and never need durability; PostgreSQL wins when the projection read model must be queryable by external services via SQL joins without an additional service boundary.
ADR-16 Dependency policy Minimal curated deps; no transitive dep on openssl by default (use rustls) Permissive transitive deps Avoid supply-chain surface; compile time; no C deps in core Permissive deps win when developer velocity and ecosystem breadth are the priority (e.g., rapid prototyping, internal tooling where supply-chain auditing is not required).
ADR-17 TLS rustls openssl, native-tls Pure Rust; no C FFI; FIPS-adjacent path via aws-lc-rs openssl wins in FIPS-140-2 certified environments where the only approved path is an OpenSSL FIPS module (US federal, healthcare, finance); native-tls wins on macOS/Windows where integrating with the OS certificate store is a hard requirement (enterprise MDM).
ADR-18 Logging tracing crate (structured, async-aware) log + env_logger, slog Span-based; integrates with tokio; pluggable subscriber (JSON for prod) log + env_logger wins for simple CLI tools and libraries where async span context is unnecessary and binary size matters; slog wins in high-frequency logging scenarios where compile-time key-value drain selection (no dynamic dispatch) shaves measurable nanoseconds per log call.
ADR-19 Metrics metrics crate + prometheus exporter OpenTelemetry metrics, statsd Lightweight; runtime-agnostic registry; Prometheus scrape standard OpenTelemetry metrics win when the observability pipeline must emit metrics, traces, and logs through a single vendor-neutral SDK to multiple backends (Datadog, Honeycomb, Grafana Cloud) without per-backend exporters; statsd wins in legacy environments where Graphite/InfluxDB pipelines already exist.
ADR-20 Distributed tracing opentelemetry-rust + OTLP exporter Jaeger native, Zipkin Vendor-neutral; correlates gRPC spans to Raft and log I/O Jaeger-native wins when the deployment mandates Jaeger's internal data model (baggage, references) and OTLP conversion loses fidelity; Zipkin wins in Java-heavy microservice environments where the ecosystem already standardizes on Zipkin B3 propagation headers.
ADR-21 Testing framework cargo test (unit/integration); proptest (property); tokio-test pytest, JUnit, criterion alone Proptest: QuickCheck-style; cargo test: zero config; criterion: micro-benchmarks pytest wins for cross-language protocol conformance testing (e.g., testing the gRPC server from a Python test harness using the official Python client); JUnit wins in Java-centric organizations sharing a single test infrastructure with Spring-based services.
ADR-22 Fuzzing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) on segment reader, framing parser, concurrency paths AFL++, honggfuzz Native Rust integration; coverage-guided; Phase 14 crash-recovery fuzz AFL++ wins when multi-language fuzzing (C FFI boundary + Rust) is needed because AFL++ has broader language support and QEMU-based binary fuzzing for closed-source components; honggfuzz wins for persistence-mode fuzzing with custom mutators requiring tighter control than libFuzzer's API.
ADR-23 Global position across partitions Per-partition independent position space; $all merge by position Single global position arbiter, hybrid logical clocks Arbiter = bottleneck; merge-sort is linearizable per-partition; cross-partition total order is advisory A global arbiter wins when strict total order across all partitions is a hard client requirement (e.g., external auditing systems that must process every event in a single deterministic sequence); HLC (Hybrid Logical Clocks) wins for geo-distributed multi-datacenter deployments where a central arbiter's RTT is unacceptable.
ADR-24 Stream partitioning hash FNV-1a 64-bit of stream_id bytes mod num_partitions Consistent hashing (rendezvous), jump hash Simple; predictable; rebalancing is explicit reassignment, not ring movement Consistent hashing (rendezvous) wins when partition count changes frequently (scaling up/down in a cloud environment) and minimizing key remapping on topology change is critical; jump hash wins when partition count is known to be a power-of-two and uniform distribution across hardware is more important than minimal remapping.
ADR-25 Benchmark harness criterion (microbenchmarks) + custom throughput bench (append/read p99) perf, hyperfine Criterion: stable; statistical confidence intervals; HTML reports perf + flamegraphs win when the primary goal is identifying hardware-level bottlenecks (cache misses, branch mispredictions) rather than tracking regression in a CI gate; hyperfine wins for command-line tool benchmarking where process-spawn overhead is the measurement target.
ADR-26 CI/CD GitHub Actions; matrix over (stable, beta Rust); cargo clippy --deny warnings; cargo test Jenkins, CircleCI Standard open-source choice; caching via actions/cache Jenkins wins in air-gapped on-premises environments where GitHub Actions runners cannot reach the internet and a self-hosted CI server is required; CircleCI wins when Docker layer caching and parallelism controls are more sophisticated than GitHub Actions' current cache API.
ADR-27 Container packaging Docker multi-stage (builder = rust:slim, runtime = distroless/debian-slim) Alpine, nix Multi-stage: small image; distroless: minimal attack surface Alpine wins when image size is the single priority and musl-libc compatibility is guaranteed (no glibc dynamic linking); Nix wins for fully reproducible builds with hermetic dependencies where the Nix store hash must match across all environments including developer laptops.
ADR-28 Licensing MIT OR Apache-2.0 (dual) GPL, BSL Rust ecosystem standard; permissive for downstream use GPL wins when the goal is to require all derivative works to be open-source (copyleft protection for a community project); BSL (Business Source License) wins for a commercial event store that wants to prevent cloud providers from offering it as a managed service without a commercial agreement.
ADR-29 Build reproducibility Cargo.lock committed; MSRV in Cargo.toml; pinned Docker base image digest Floating toolchain Deterministic builds across CI and developer machines Floating toolchain wins during rapid prototyping when always-latest dependencies are desired and reproducibility is not yet a requirement; this should be converted to pinned before any production release.
ADR-30 Scavenge algorithm Offline segment rewrite with live-range bitmap (RoaringBitmap) In-place edit, log-structured merge Append-only constraint preserved; bitmap compact; online scavenge via background task with pause In-place edit wins in space-constrained environments where writing a second copy of the segment before swapping is impossible (disk < 2× segment size); log-structured merge (Kafka-style compaction by key) wins when the workload is key-value with updates (keep-last semantics) rather than append-only event sourcing.
