T15 Disk exhaustion esdb-log segment writer Ingest rate exceeds scavenge rate; no capacity planning Disk-watermark: reject appends at 95% full with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED; alert at 80% esdb_disk_free_bytes gauge Alert fires at 80% and 95% Trigger scavenge; add disk; throttle ingest (RB-03)
T16 Replay non-determinism exploit esdb-projection handler Non-pure handler reads external state (HTTP call, random seed) Projection API surfaces only event data + metadata; no outbound I/O allowed in handler context; enforced by API design Divergence in Phase 14 replay-equivalence test I8 conformance test result Fix handler to be pure; rebuild projection
T17 TLS certificate expiry inter-node mTLS Expired cert causes cluster fragmentation Cert rotation automation; alert on cert expiry < 30 days TLS handshake failure esdb_tls_cert_expiry_days{node} Rotate cert; rolling restart
T18 Replay of captured append request (replay attack) gRPC channel Attacker replays a captured append RPC Idempotent-append dedup by EventId (I6); TLS with replay-nonce; short JWT TTL Duplicate EventId on second append → noop esdb_duplicate_event_id_total No duplicate written; I6 invariant holds
T19 Projection state exfiltration Admin / project API Unauthorized read of projection state (read model may contain PII) ACL on projection read endpoint; mTLS for admin API Unauthorized read counter esdb_auth_failures_total{operation=read_projection} Revoke token; audit who accessed which projection
T20 Log compaction race — live events removed esdb-storage scavenge + concurrent append Scavenge runs while append is in flight; live event appears deleted Scavenge holds read lock on segment boundary; events committed before scavenge-start are protected by ScavengePlan live-range (I10) Post-scavenge conformance scan esdb_scavenge_live_events_skipped_total Retry conformance scan; restore from backup if violation detected

Failure Modes & Recovery

Failure Classification Axes

Each failure mode is characterized along four axes: scope (single-node vs cluster-wide vs data-plane), detectability (immediate vs delayed vs latent), recoverability (automatic vs operator-assisted vs requires backup), and data-safety impact (none / degraded reads / lost writes / lost committed data). Committed-event loss is a P0 scenario; the invariants I1–I10 and Raft safety (I7) exist precisely to make it structurally impossible under correct operation.

Failure Taxonomy Table

ID Failure Class Symptoms Root Causes Detection Auto Recovery Manual Recovery Operator Actions Forensic Artifacts Long-term Mitigation
F01 Torn write / partial append CRC error on restart; truncated tail in last segment; log: "torn tail detected" Process SIGKILL mid-fsync; power loss before fsync completes CRC mismatch during startup segment scan Truncate to last valid CRC; rebuild per-stream and global-position index from raw log (P8, Phase 2, Phase 9) None if auto succeeds; if auto fails restore from replica or backup (RB-13) Verify esdb_log_truncations_total; inspect segment boundary offset Last valid segment file + offset; truncation event log WAL fsync discipline; O_DSYNC on segment writer; segment CRC (Phase 2)
F02 WrongExpectedVersion surge High esdb_wrong_expected_version_total; client 409-equivalent responses; write throughput drop Hot-path aggregate contention; client retry storm without jitter OCC metric rate spike > baseline × 10 Back-off response header sent by esdb-server; I5 prevents any partial write Add stream-level category partitioning; tune client retry with jitter Check aggregate write concurrency model; profile hot stream_id Per-stream version counter in log; OCC metric time-series Partition hot aggregates (Phase 12); client retry jitter; command deduplication
F03 Subscription lag buildup esdb_sub_lag_positions rising; consumer processing stalls; ack rate drops to zero Slow consumer handler; network partition to consumer process; consumer crash Lag metric > SLO threshold (100 000 positions for 15 min) Ack timeout → nack → retry queue; competing consumer in group picks up work (Phase 13) Restart consumer group; increase consumer parallelism esdb-cli sub status; inspect park queue depth; check ack/nack ratio Checkpoint position; nack log; park queue dump Competing consumers (Phase 13); backpressure tokens; handler performance profiling
F04 Projection fault / stall Projection status == Faulted; checkpoint not advancing; read model stale Handler panic; schema mismatch in event payload; OOM in handler; non-pure handler (external call timeout) esdb_projection_faults_total > 0; checkpoint age metric exceeds threshold Projection isolated; server continues; other projections unaffected Fix handler code; issue esdb-cli project rebuild --name <n> (resets checkpoint to 0) Inspect fault log: projection name, position, error, backtrace Fault log entry: projection, position, panic message Handler isolation per task; schema versioning in EventRecord (Phase 1); I8 conformance test
F05 Raft leader loss No appends accepted; clients receive UNAVAILABLE or redirect to no-leader; esdb_raft_role shows no Leader node Leader node crash; network partition isolates leader; GC pause exceeds election timeout Heartbeat timeout on followers triggers election; esdb_raft_role transition to Candidate Followers elect new leader within 2× election timeout (randomized timeout, Phase 11) None required if quorum intact; verify via health endpoint Confirm quorum size; check all node health endpoints; redirect clients after election Term log; vote records; last commit_index on each node Randomized election timeouts; tuned heartbeat interval < 150 ms; Phase 11

F09 Snapshot schema mismatch Snapshot load rejected; full replay forced; high startup time for large streams Aggregate schema evolved without snapshot version bump; incompatible Bytes encoding esdb_snapshot_load_failures_total > 0; startup replay time spike Fall back to full stream replay; write fresh snapshot after load (I9, Phase 6) Delete stale snapshots for affected stream; re-run aggregate load esdb-cli snapshot list --stream <s>; delete old versions Snapshot schema_version field; error log with expected vs actual version Snapshot schema versioning; compatibility matrix per aggregate type
F10 Disk full — append blocked Appends rejected with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED; log segment roll-over fails Ingest rate exceeds scavenge rate; insufficient disk provisioning esdb_disk_free_bytes < 5%; alert fires at 20% None — appends blocked until space freed Trigger scavenge; delete soft-deleted streams; add disk volume esdb-cli scavenge start --dry-run first; then --execute; monitor progress Disk usage history; segment count per node Disk watermark alerts at 80% and 95%; capacity planning runbook (RB-03)

Operational Readiness

Metrics Strategy

All metrics exported as Prometheus text format via /metrics HTTP endpoint on every esdb-server instance. Labels kept low-cardinality; stream_id label used only for error/OCC metrics, never for per-event counters. Histogram buckets aligned to SLO thresholds.

esdb_append_duration_seconds Histogram node p50/p99/p999 append latency; SLO anchor

esdb_duplicate_event_id_total Counter node Idempotent re-append rate (I6)
esdb_log_bytes_written_total Counter segment, node Write amplification, disk budget
esdb_log_truncations_total Counter node Torn-write recovery frequency
esdb_crc_errors_total Counter node, segment Data integrity violations; P1 alert

esdb_disk_free_bytes Gauge node, volume Capacity — alert at 20%
esdb_sub_lag_positions Gauge subscription_id Positions behind global head

Tracing Strategy

  • Framework: opentelemetry crate + tracing-opentelemetry bridge; OTLP exporter to Jaeger or Grafana Tempo.
  • Propagation: W3C traceparent / tracestate headers on all gRPC and HTTP requests.
  • Span hierarchy per append: append_request → auth_check → partition_route → concurrency_check → log_write → raft_replicate → fsync → commit_ack.
  • Span attributes (mandatory): stream_id, event_count, expected_version, node_id, raft_term, global_position (on commit).
  • Async propagation: span context carried across tokio::spawn via tracing instrument macros; no context loss at async boundaries.
  • Sampling: 100% for errors and latency > p99 SLO; head-based 1% for normal path; tail-based sampling enabled in collector for p99 outliers.

Structured Logging

  • Format: JSON via tracing-subscriber + tracing-json; level-filtered at runtime (ESDB_LOG env var).
  • Mandatory fields on every line: ts (RFC3339), level, node_id, trace_id, span_id, target (crate::module), message.
  • Operation fields: stream_id, event_number, global_position, subscription_id, projection_name, raft_term, peer_ip, error_code.
  • Log level policy: ERROR — unrecoverable / data-integrity (CRC, torn write); WARN — retryable / degraded (OCC, nack, snapshot fallback); INFO — lifecycle (startup, leader election, scavenge start/end, projection rebuild); DEBUG — per-event path (disabled in production); TRACE — Raft message detail (development only).
  • Log shipping: stdout → vector / fluentbit → Loki or Elasticsearch; retention ≥ 30 days.

Dashboard Design

Dashboard Key Panels
Cluster Health esdb_cluster_healthy; Raft role per node (color-coded); commit_index delta between nodes; election events timeline; term rate
Ingest Throughput Appends/sec by category; append p99 latency (SLO line); WrongExpectedVersion rate; batch size distribution
Replication Per-follower commit_index delta from leader; replication latency histogram; leader identity
Subscriptions Lag by subscription_id (position units); ack/nack rate; park queue depth; active competing consumer count
Projections Lag per projection; checkpoint age; fault count; rebuild-in-progress indicator; state size
Storage Disk used/free per node; segment count; index rebuild duration; CRC error rate; scavenge bytes removed
Security Auth failure rate by peer_ip; cert expiry days; oversized-payload rejections; clock skew
Error Overview Error log stream (tail); alert firing state; P1 vs P2 breakdown; MTTR rolling average

Alerting Philosophy

Fire on SLO-burn rate (multi-window: 1 h fast burn + 6 h slow burn); not raw instantaneous thresholds. Every alert links to its runbook ID. Silence applied during maintenance windows declared in PagerDuty. Three tiers: P1 = page on-call within 5 min; P2 = ticket within 1 h; P3 = dashboard / next business day.

Alert Name Tier Firing Condition Runbook
ClusterUnhealthy P1 esdb_cluster_healthy == 0 for > 30 s RB-01
AppendLatencyHigh P1 Append p99 > 200 ms for 5 min sustained RB-02
DiskUsageCritical P1 esdb_disk_free_bytes < 5% RB-03
CRCError P1 esdb_crc_errors_total rate > 0 (any) RB-07
ReplicationLagHigh P2 Follower commit_index delta > 10 000 positions for 10 min RB-04
SubscriptionLagHigh P2 esdb_sub_lag_positions > 100 000 for 15 min RB-05
ProjectionFault P2 esdb_projection_faults_total rate > 0 RB-06
DiskUsageWarning P2 esdb_disk_free_bytes < 20% RB-03
AuthFailureSpike P2 Auth failure rate > 100 / min for 3 min RB-08
ClockSkewHigh P2 esdb_clock_skew_ms p99 > 500 ms for 5 min RB-09
TLSCertExpiringSoon P2 esdb_tls_cert_expiry_days < 30 RB-12
IndexRebuildSlow P3 esdb_index_rebuild_seconds p99 > 300 s RB-07

SLO Definitions

SLO Target Measurement Window Error Budget Burn-rate Alert
Append availability 99.9% 30 days rolling 43.8 min / month 1 h fast + 6 h slow
Append p99 latency < 50 ms 5-min rolling average Burn-rate on > 200 ms Per AppendLatencyHigh alert
Committed event durability 100.0% (I7) All time Zero tolerance; any rollback = P0 Invariant test in Phase 14
Persistent subscription delivery ≥ 99.9% at-least-once 30 days 43.8 min / month lost delivery SubscriptionLagHigh proxy
Read availability 99.95% 30 days 21.9 min / month ClusterUnhealthy + read-error rate
Projection checkpoint freshness Lag < 30 s under normal load 30 days 99.9% of 5-min windows in-SLO ProjectionLagHigh
Data integrity (no CRC error) 100% All time Zero tolerance CRCError P1 alert

Incident Response

  1. Alert fires → PagerDuty pages on-call; auto-post to #esdb-incidents Slack channel.
  2. Acknowledge within SLA (P1: 5 min; P2: 1 h); open incident thread with Grafana snapshot link.
  3. Run named runbook; attach Jaeger trace for the affected time window.
  4. Mitigation first; RCA drafted within 24 h (P1) or 72 h (P2).
  5. Post-mortem published; update runbook if gap found; file follow-up tickets.

Runbooks

ID Title Trigger
RB-01 Cluster quorum loss: restore quorum or single-node recovery procedure ClusterUnhealthy P1
RB-02 Append latency degradation: diagnose fsync stall, Raft pipeline, disk I/O AppendLatencyHigh P1
RB-03 Disk full: trigger scavenge, add volume, throttle ingest DiskUsageCritical P1 / DiskUsageWarning P2
RB-04 Replication lag: identify slow follower, network bottleneck, or index rebuild in progress ReplicationLagHigh P2
RB-05 Subscription lag: scale competing consumers, inspect park queue, increase ack window SubscriptionLagHigh P2
RB-06 Projection fault: inspect handler panic, fix, rebuild or disable projection ProjectionFault P2
RB-07 CRC / index error: identify corrupt segment, restore from replica or backup, rebuild index CRCError P1 / IndexRebuildSlow P3
RB-08 Auth failure spike: identify source IP, revoke token, apply rate-limit rule AuthFailureSpike P2
RB-09 Clock skew: re-sync NTP, check VM clock source, verify TLS cert validity ClockSkewHigh P2
RB-10 Scavenge: dry-run validation, backup pre-scavenge, execute, post-scavenge conformance scan Operator-initiated
RB-11 Snapshot schema mismatch: list stale snapshots, delete, trigger full replay, write new snapshot Operator / ProjectionFault adjacent
RB-12 Rolling upgrade: node-by-node restart, quorum check after each step, rollback if P1 fires Deployment
RB-13 Backup / restore: segment tar + index + snapshot export; restore procedure; verify conformance DiskUsageCritical or data-loss event

Backup / Restore Strategy

  • Backup unit: complete set of segment files + index files + snapshot store per node; Checkpoint table for subscriptions and projections.
  • Method: esdb-cli backup snapshot --output s3://bucket/node-<id>/$(date) — quorum-read ensures backup reflects only committed events (I7).
  • Continuous WAL shipping: on each segment roll-over, upload closed segment to object storage; achieves near-continuous incremental backup.
  • Frequency: daily full backup; continuous segment shipping for point-in-time recovery.
  • Restore: copy segments + index to data directory; restart node; node discovers commit_index, applies Raft log tail to catch up, or bootstraps single-node from backup.
  • RTO target: < 1 h for single-node restore from most-recent segment backup.
  • RPO target: < 5 min with continuous segment shipping; < 24 h with daily-only backup.
  • Validation: automated restore-and-replay conformance test weekly in staging environment; compares event count, positions, and a sampled read-model state.

Deployment Topology

  • Minimum production: 3-node cluster (Raft quorum = 2); 5-node recommended for zero-quorum-reduction rolling upgrades.
  • Node sizing: NVMe SSD required for log volume (fsync latency < 1 ms target); separate volume for index, snapshots, and Raft log.
  • Load balancer (esdb-server): route AppendEvents to current leader (gossip-discovered); route ReadStream / ReadAll to any node (with optional require_leader=true for linearizable reads).
  • Multi-datacenter: 3 nodes across 2 DCs; 5 nodes across 3 DCs for single-DC failure tolerance with quorum preserved.
  • Partitioned deployments (Phase 12): each partition group is a separate 3- or 5-node Raft cluster; esdb-partition routes at the server layer.

Rolling Upgrade Procedure

  1. Upgrade one follower (stop, replace binary, start); verify esdb_cluster_healthy == 1 before proceeding.
  2. Repeat for remaining followers.
  3. Trigger leader stepdown (esdb-cli cluster stepdown); upgraded follower becomes leader.
  4. Upgrade former leader node.
  5. Verify: esdb_cluster_healthy == 1; esdb_raft_role shows expected single Leader; no P1 alerts within 5-min observation window.
  6. Rollback: reverse order; old binary is wire-compatible within one MINOR version window (see Compatibility & Versioning).

Canary Deployment

  • Deploy new version to 1 node; route 5% of read traffic via load-balancer weight.
  • Observe esdb_append_duration_seconds, esdb_crc_errors_total, esdb_cluster_healthy for 30 min.
  • Promote to full rollout on no SLO burn; abort + rollback if any P1 alert fires or append error rate increases.

Migration Strategy

Migration Type Trigger Procedure Validation
Segment format version bump (MAJOR) framing change Old node drains; new node writes new format; backward-compat read path for old segments until all rewritten by scavenge esdb-cli storage verify --all-segments
Index format version bump index schema change On startup, new binary detects old version header → auto-rebuild index Index rebuild duration metric; read conformance spot-check
Checkpoint format change (MINOR additive) new checkpoint fields New fields default-initialized from GlobalPosition; no migration script needed Subscription delivery continuity test
Checkpoint format change (MAJOR) field semantics change Release migration script; run before upgrading any node; checkpoints converted in-place Re-verify subscription lag after migration
Projection query DSL change (MAJOR) syntax break Export current projection state; rewrite query; import state or rebuild Replay-equivalence test (I8, Phase 14)
Raft log entry layout (MAJOR) ReplicationEntry shape Drain all in-flight commits on leader; verify commit_index == last_log_index; upgrade followers then leader Raft safety test: no committed event lost post-upgrade

Capacity Planning Guidelines

Resource Signal Action Threshold Action
Disk — log volume esdb_disk_used_bytes / total 70% full Plan scavenge or add volume within 1 sprint
Disk — log volume esdb_disk_free_bytes < 20% Alert P2; schedule scavenge
Disk — log volume esdb_disk_free_bytes < 5% Alert P1; block new appends
Append throughput esdb_appends_total rate > 80% of tested max Add partitions (Phase 12) or increase hardware
Subscription consumer lag esdb_sub_lag_positions Trending up over 1 h Add competing consumers; profile handler
Projection lag esdb_projection_lag_positions > 30 s wall-clock equivalent Optimize handler; enable parallel catch-up mode
Raft election frequency esdb_raft_term_total rate > 1 election / 10 min Investigate leader stability; tune timeouts
Index rebuild duration esdb_index_rebuild_seconds > 120 s Add index checkpoint frequency; increase IO priority

On-Call Escalation Matrix

Condition First Responder Escalation Time-box Before Escalate
P1 ClusterUnhealthy On-call SRE Platform lead + DB team 15 min without mitigation
P1 CRCError On-call SRE Storage / infra lead 10 min; immediately if on primary node
P1 AppendLatencyHigh On-call SRE DB team 20 min
P2 ProjectionFault On-call engineer Owning team (projection owner) 1 h
P2 ReplicationLag On-call SRE DB team 2 h
P0 Committed event loss (post-mortem) Incident commander Engineering VP + legal Immediately on confirmation

Observability Integration Points

  • Changefeed / CDC: esdb-subscribe emits events to Kafka/Kinesis adapter via competing-consumer subscription; downstream CDC consumers use GlobalPosition as their cursor (I3).
  • APM integration: OpenTelemetry traces correlate across app → esdb-server → Raft → disk; trace_id propagated in EventRecord.metadata standard envelope field for end-to-end correlation.
  • Health endpoint: GET /health returns cluster quorum status, leader identity, disk free, and projection lag summary as JSON; used by load balancer health checks and Kubernetes liveness/readiness probes.
  • Gossip / cluster info: GET /cluster returns per-node role, commit_index, match_index, version; used by esdb-cli cluster status and dashboard.
  • Audit log: structured JSON append to separate audit sink; records: stream tombstone, scavenge execution, admin API calls, auth failures, operator identity; immutable, separate from application log.

Compatibility & Versioning

Versioning Policy

Semantic versioning MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH: MAJOR = breaking wire, storage, or API change; MINOR = additive wire / storage / API change; PATCH = bug fix, no protocol change. Public API surface = all items in esdb-core, esdb-event, esdb-server gRPC proto, and esdb-cli subcommand interface.

Wire Compatibility (gRPC / Protobuf)

Change Type Classification Guarantee
Add optional proto field MINOR Backward-compatible; old decoders ignore unknown fields
Remove proto field MAJOR Deprecation cycle ≥ 2 MINOR releases; deprecated field returns default for 1 MINOR cycle before removal
Rename proto field MAJOR Treated as remove + add; no rename shortcut
Add RPC method MINOR Old clients unaffected
Remove RPC method MAJOR Deprecated RPC returns UNIMPLEMENTED for ≥ 1 MINOR cycle
Add enum value MINOR Old decoders receive unknown variant; must handle gracefully
Remove enum value MAJOR
EventRecord.data / .metadata wire type Stable across all MINOR Opaque bytes; store never re-encodes payload
ExpectedVersion proto encoding MAJOR to change variant encoding Existing variants never reinterpreted
GlobalPosition wire type (uint64) Stable Strictly increasing; safe to persist externally as a cursor (I3)

Storage Format Compatibility

Component Format Anchor Compatibility Rule
Segment files Length-prefix (4 B) + CRC32C (4 B) + framed serialized EventRecord MAJOR to change framing; old segments readable by new binary within MAJOR; format version byte in segment header
Index files (StreamId, EventNumber) → file_offset (u64) + GlobalPosition → file_offset (u64) Version header in index file; mismatch triggers automatic rebuild from segments on startup
Snapshot store (StreamId, EventNumber) → {schema_version: u32, state: Bytes} schema_version checked on load; mismatch falls back to full replay (I9); snapshots are advisory, never normative for correctness
Raft log entries on disk ReplicationEntry{position: GlobalPosition, term: u64, event_record} MAJOR to change layout; upgrade path: drain all pending commits (commit_index == last_log_index), then upgrade; new binary reads old layout via explicit migration path in RB-12
Checkpoint store {subscription_or_projection_id, position: GlobalPosition} Additive fields MINOR-compatible; MAJOR requires checkpoint migration script provided in release notes
ScavengePlan artifact JSON manifest of (segment_path, live-ranges[]) Not a persistent store; regenerated on each scavenge run; no compatibility constraint

Type and Schema Evolution

Type Field / Aspect Evolution Rule
EventRecord data: Bytes, metadata: Bytes Opaque; store never interprets; version in event_type string (application-owned); schema evolution is the application's responsibility
EventRecord event_type: String Additive new type strings MINOR; removing handling for old type strings is application-owned
EventRecord event_id, stream, number, position, created Immutable post-commit (I1); never updated in store
ExpectedVersion Enum variants New variants are MAJOR; existing variant semantics never reinterpreted
GlobalPosition u64 Strictly monotonic, never reused (I3); external systems may persist as cursor
EventNumber u64 Zero-based, gap-free per stream (I2); external systems may persist as cursor
Snapshot.state Bytes Application-owned schema; schema_version field in Snapshot struct for load-time compatibility check
Projection query DSL Syntax Additive syntax constructs MINOR; breaking syntax change MAJOR with explicit migration guide
Checkpoint.position GlobalPosition Monotonically advances; never decremented; backward-compatible across MINOR

API Stability Tiers

Tier Scope Guarantee
Stable AppendEvents, ReadStream, ReadAll, Subscribe, Ack, Nack RPCs; all EventRecord fields; AppendResult; ReadResult No breaking change within a MAJOR version
Beta Projection query DSL; Admin / scavenge HTTP API; partition routing API; cluster management RPCs May change in MINOR with deprecation notice ≥ 1 release
Experimental Internal cluster gossip wire format; esdb-cli flag names (non---help surface); esdb-replication internal RPC No guarantee; may change in PATCH

Rolling-Upgrade Guarantee

Within a MINOR version band: a cluster may run version N and N+1 binaries simultaneously during a rolling upgrade with no data loss, no downtime, and no append stall beyond a leader-election window. Both binaries must be able to read and write segments and index files produced by the other. Cross-MAJOR upgrade requires: (1) all nodes at latest MINOR of current MAJOR first; (2) drain all in-flight appends (verify commit_index == last_log_index on leader); (3) follow documented migration runbook (RB-12).

Deprecation Policy

  1. Mark deprecated in proto with [deprecated = true]; document in release notes.
  2. Maintain deprecated item for ≥ 2 MINOR releases or 6 months (whichever is longer).
  3. Remove in the next MAJOR version with a migration guide referencing the replacement.
  4. Deprecated RPCs return UNIMPLEMENTED for exactly one MINOR cycle before removal.

Cross-Reference to Domain Model (Section 8) and Core Types (Section 11)

  • EventRecord, GlobalPosition, EventNumber, Checkpoint, Snapshot, ReplicationEntry — all defined in Section 8 (Domain Model) and typed in Section 11 (Core Types); their wire-serialized forms follow the gRPC compatibility table above; their on-disk forms follow the storage format table above.
  • StreamId, EventId, ExpectedVersion, AppendResult, ReadDirection, Subscription, Projection — public API types in esdb-core and esdb-event; Rust type shapes are MAJOR-version-stable in the crate's pub surface.
  • Segment / IndexEntry format anchored in esdb-log (Phase 2) and hardened in esdb-storage (Phase 9) is the single storage compatibility reference; all other persistence layers (snapshots, checkpoints, Raft log) reference or wrap it.
  • GlobalPosition is the universal cursor for subscriptions, projections, replication, and external consumers; its monotonicity (I3) and uniqueness guarantees are the foundation of all compatibility promises for externally persisted cursors.

Semantic Versioning Contract for Crate Boundaries

Crate Public Surface Version Contract
esdb-core StreamId, EventId, EventNumber, GlobalPosition, ExpectedVersion Stable; MAJOR only for type layout changes
esdb-event EventRecord, Snapshot, Checkpoint Stable; MAJOR for field removal or type changes
esdb-log Segment format, IndexEntry Beta; format version header in segment; MINOR for additive index fields
esdb-server gRPC proto esdb.proto Stable Tier per table above
esdb-replication Internal Raft RPC Experimental; no cross-version guarantee
esdb-cli Subcommand names and required flags Beta; MINOR may add flags, MAJOR may rename subcommands

External Cursor Stability

External consumers (downstream systems, CDC adapters, projection clients) that persist GlobalPosition or EventNumber as cursors are protected by the following guarantees:

Cursor Type Persistence Safety Guarantee Caveat
GlobalPosition Safe to persist externally Strictly monotonic; unique; never reused (I3); always valid as a resume point for $all reads Position gaps are possible (e.g., between prepare and commit positions); readers must tolerate forward-only advancement
EventNumber (per stream) Safe to persist externally Zero-based, gap-free within a stream (I2); never reused even after tombstone After stream scavenge, deleted event numbers are permanently absent; read from that number returns next live event or end
Projection Checkpoint Safe to persist externally Monotonically advancing GlobalPosition; at-least-once delivery means re-processing from checkpoint is safe if handler is idempotent (P5) Checkpoint must be durably written before handler side-effects are finalized
Subscription Checkpoint Safe to persist externally Same as Projection Checkpoint; persistent subscriptions maintain server-side checkpoint in addition Volatile subscriptions have no server-side checkpoint; caller must track position independently

Protocol Negotiation and Versioning Headers

  • gRPC: esdb-server-version response header on all RPCs; client may inspect to detect MAJOR mismatch.
  • HTTP REST (admin): X-ESDB-Version response header; Accept-Version request header for version negotiation.
  • Cluster gossip: version field in each gossip message; nodes reject gossip from peers with incompatible MAJOR version.
  • Segment files: 8-byte magic (ESDBSEG\0) + 4-byte format version at byte offset 0 of every segment; reader checks before parsing.
  • Index files: 8-byte magic (ESDBIDX\0) + 4-byte format version at offset 0; mismatch triggers rebuild, never silent misread.
  • Raft log entries: format_version: u8 in ReplicationEntry; allows in-log migration without MAJOR bump for minor additions.

Guarantees NOT Made

Non-guarantee Rationale
Causal ordering across streams The store provides total order in $all by commit position, not by application causality; applications must embed causal metadata in EventRecord.metadata if needed
Exactly-once append (no idempotency key) Without an EventId, repeated appends create duplicates; EventId is the caller's responsibility (I6)
Sub-millisecond append latency fsync and Raft pipeline impose a floor; p50 target is < 10 ms, p99 < 50 ms under normal load
Read-your-writes without leader routing A read from a follower without require_leader=true may not reflect the most recently committed append
Projection state portability across MAJOR versions Projection state (Bytes) is application-owned; store provides the log but does not migrate read-model state across schema versions
Scavenge reversibility Once a segment is rewritten by scavenge, removed events are gone; backup is the only recovery path

Testing & Verification Strategy

Unit Testing

Crate What How
esdb-core StreamId parsing, category derivation, EventId uniqueness, ExpectedVersion matching #[test] in-crate; property: roundtrip serde on EventRecord
esdb-log CRC computation, length-prefix framing, offset arithmetic, segment roll-over boundaries Single-segment in-memory tempdir; inject bitflip into CRC byte → verify rejection
esdb-stream ExpectedVersion all four variants, WrongExpectedVersion rejection, EventId dedup (I6), gap-free numbering (I2) In-memory log stub; parametric over all ExpectedVersion arms
esdb-snapshot Snapshot write/load, nearest-snapshot selection, tail-replay equivalence (I9) Fix stream at N events; snapshot at K; compare fold(all) vs snap+tail
esdb-projection Handler idempotency, checkpoint persistence, state determinism (I8) Feed events twice; assert state identical; corrupt checkpoint → assert rebuild from 0
esdb-subscribe Catch-up→live boundary: no gap, no duplicate at transition Two-thread: writer races subscriber; collect all events; assert contiguous EventNumbers
esdb-replication ReplicationEntry framing, term comparison, match_index advance, commit_index quorum math 3-node in-process mock; drive AppendEntries / vote RPCs directly
esdb-partition Hash routing stability, ownership lookup, cross-partition $all merge order 16-partition ring; assert StreamId → same partition across re-seeds
esdb-storage Segment rewrite preserves live-event positions/numbers (I10) Build segment, mark 3 events deleted, scavenge, assert remaining events unchanged

Integration Testing

  • Append + read roundtrip: append 1 000 events across 100 streams; read each stream forward and backward; assert EventNumbers contiguous, GlobalPositions strictly increasing (I2, I3).
  • Crash recovery: append, kill process mid-segment (SIGKILL), restart; assert no torn event visible, last complete event readable, index consistent.
  • Subscription catch-up: start subscriber at GlobalPosition(0) after 10 000 events exist; assert all events delivered in commit order before live mode.
  • Persistent subscription ack/nack/park: nack three events; assert parked queue; re-deliver on retry; ack; assert checkpoint advances.
  • Projection rebuild: build read model, drop it, replay from 0; assert identical state (I8).
  • Scavenge live: append, tombstone stream, scavenge; verify deleted events absent in subsequent reads; verify other streams intact (I10).
  • Cluster append quorum: 3-node cluster; append 500 events; kill follower; append 500 more; restart follower; assert follower converges to full 1 000, commit positions identical.

Differential Testing (Reference Implementation)

  • Reference: EventStoreDB OSS (Go/C# binary, run via Docker in CI) for semantic equivalence on append/read/subscription.
  • Driver: shared YAML test corpus; each case specifies appends + expected reads + expected subscription event order.
  • Checks: ExpectedVersion rejection semantics must match byte-for-byte on error codes; $all order must match; persistent subscription delivery order must match.
  • Divergence log: record any semantic divergence in tests/differential/divergences.md with justification (intentional extension vs. bug).
  • Scope: Phases 3, 5, 7, 13 gated by differential suite green.

Property-Based Testing

Property Generator Assertion
EventRecord serde roundtrip arbitrary EventRecord (random stream, uuid, bytes) decode(encode(r)) == r
Per-stream contiguity (I2) arbitrary interleaved appends across N streams numbers in each stream are 0..len-1
GlobalPosition monotonicity (I3) arbitrary append sequence positions strictly increasing in $all
Snapshot+tail == full replay (I9) random stream length K, snapshot at random J ≤ K fold(0..K) == apply(snap_j, J..K)
Scavenge idempotency random live/delete pattern; scavenge twice second scavenge produces identical segment
Optimistic concurrency (I5) two goroutines racing on same stream, same expected version exactly one succeeds, one gets WrongExpectedVersion

Tool: proptest crate; 10 000 iterations per property in CI; seed logged for reproduction.

Fuzzing

Target Fuzzer Corpus Abort condition
Segment record parsing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) valid segment bytes + mutations panic, memory unsafety, CRC accept on corrupt
gRPC request parsing (esdb-server) libFuzzer over raw bytes → prost decode valid proto frames panic, OOM
Raft message deserialization libFuzzer valid ReplicationEntry bytes panic, invariant violation
Scavenge plan application libFuzzer over ScavengePlan + segment valid inputs position/number mismatch on output

Fuzz corpora stored in fuzz/corpus/; reproduce failing input with cargo fuzz run <target> <artifact>.

Chaos Testing

  • Tool: toxiproxy for network; kill -9 on follower processes; disk-full injection via fallocate.
  • Scenarios:
    • Leader killed mid-append: assert quorum commits exactly the events that were acked.
    • Network partition (3-node, minority isolated): assert minority serves stale reads, majority continues appends, no split-brain write accepted.
    • Follower lagging 10 000 events, then reconnecting: assert log matching restores without event loss.
    • Disk full on leader: assert append fails clean, no partial segment, node remains healthy after space freed.
  • Verification: after each chaos scenario, run full differential suite + invariant checker.

Deterministic Replay Testing

  • What: given a recorded sequence of appends to a stream or $all, replaying through a projection from position 0 must yield the same read-model state as incremental application (I8, P5).
  • How: record append log as a fixture file (JSON); replay engine re-applies through projection; compare final state bytes.
  • Coverage: every projection in the integration suite has a corresponding fixture; fixture is regenerated on schema change and checked into tests/fixtures/.

Soak / Endurance Testing

  • Duration: 6 h at 50% peak write throughput.
  • Metrics collected: heap RSS (must not grow unboundedly), segment file count (must roll deterministically), subscription lag (must stay < 1 s at steady state), p99 append latency (must not degrade > 20% from baseline after 1 h).
  • Failure trigger: RSS growth > 200 MB over baseline after 1 h; p99 latency regression > 20%; any panic or invariant violation.

Fault Injection

  • fail crate (failpoints) at: post-segment-write pre-fsync; post-leader-append pre-replicate; post-quorum pre-ack; post-snapshot-write pre-checkpoint.
  • Each failpoint exercised: crash → restart → verify recovery; assert no torn event, no duplicate commit.

Conformance Validation

  • Invariant checker binary (esdb-verify): scans all segments, verifies I1–I10 exhaustively; run after every integration scenario.
  • CI gate: esdb-verify must exit 0 before any merge to main.

Performance Regression

  • Benchmark suite (criterion) run on every PR; results posted as CI comment.
  • Gate: no benchmark may regress > 10% vs. main baseline; gate blocks merge.
  • Tracked benchmarks: append_1k_events_single_stream, read_stream_1k_forward, all_stream_scan_10k, subscription_catchup_100k, projection_rebuild_100k.

Build & Release Engineering

Toolchain

Item Version / Policy
Rust edition 2021
MSRV 1.78 (pinned in rust-toolchain.toml)
Toolchain file rust-toolchain.toml checked in; CI and developers use same pin
Nightly usage none in production crates; nightly only for fuzz targets via cargo-fuzz
Cross-compilation cross + Docker images for x86_64-unknown-linux-musl, aarch64-unknown-linux-musl

Reproducible Builds

  • Cargo.lock checked in; no floating dependencies.
  • Docker build image pinned by digest (not tag).
  • SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH set in CI to suppress timestamp non-determinism.
  • Binary built inside hermetic container; host toolchain never touches artifacts.
  • Verify: two CI runs on the same commit must produce bit-identical binaries (checked by SHA-256 comparison step).

Artifact Signing and Provenance

  • Release binaries signed with sigstore/cosign; signature + SLSA provenance attestation uploaded to OCI registry alongside image.
  • SLSA level 2: build on ephemeral CI runners, provenance generated by slsa-github-generator.
  • Git tags signed with developer GPG key; CI rejects unsigned tags.

SBOM

  • Generated via cargo-cyclonedx (CycloneDX 1.4 JSON) on every release.
  • SBOM includes all transitive dependencies, license SPDX identifiers, crate versions.
  • Published to release assets alongside binaries.

Dependency Auditing

  • cargo audit runs on every CI push; blocks merge on any RUSTSEC advisory at critical or high.
  • cargo deny enforces: allowed license list (MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD-2-Clause, BSD-3-Clause); no banned crates; no duplicate versions of the same crate (except where cargo deny allows a known exception).
  • Weekly scheduled CI job re-runs audit against latest advisory DB.

CI/CD Topology

Push → lint (clippy --deny warnings) → fmt check → unit tests (all crates)
     → integration tests (Docker compose: 3-node cluster + toxiproxy)
     → differential suite (EventStoreDB reference container)
     → property tests (10k iterations)
     → fuzz smoke (30 s per target)
     → cargo audit + cargo deny
     → benchmark regression check
     → (on tag) → cross-compile all targets → sign → SBOM → publish OCI + GitHub Release
  • Lint and unit tests run in < 5 min (parallelized per crate).
  • Integration + differential run in < 15 min (parallelized scenario groups).
  • Full fuzz run (continuous) on dedicated runner; not a merge gate.
  • Release pipeline triggered by signed semver tag; no manual promotion step.

Version Stamping

  • Version sourced from Cargo.toml; build.rs embeds GIT_COMMIT, BUILD_TIMESTAMP, CARGO_PKG_VERSION into binary via env! macros exposed on esdb-server --version.
  • Release channel encoded in version suffix: 1.2.3 = stable; 1.2.3-rc.1 = release candidate; 1.2.3-dev = nightly.

Release Channels

Channel Trigger Audience
nightly merge to main internal, CI consumers
release-candidate v*-rc.* tag integration testers
stable v* semver tag (no pre-release suffix) production

Supply-Chain Verification

  • cargo vet audits for third-party crates requiring manual review; audit records committed to supply-chain/.
  • cargo-crev used for crates touching I/O or cryptography.
  • Dependency update PRs auto-generated weekly by Dependabot; require audit annotation before merge.

Performance Engineering

Throughput Targets (from Constraints / Phase 14)

Each target is gated in CI: the named phase's benchmark or conformance suite fails the build if the target regresses.

M# Metric Target Condition Phase Gate
M1 Single-stream append throughput 50 000 events/s single node, NVMe, batch size 100 Phase 14 (throughput_bench)
M2 $all sequential read throughput 200 000 events/s from cold page cache, 256-byte avg event Phase 5, Phase 14
M3 Subscription catch-up throughput 150 000 events/s from position 0, no projection overhead Phase 7, Phase 14
M4 Projection rebuild throughput 100 000 events/s single-threaded handler, no network Phase 8, Phase 14
M5 Replication append throughput (3-node) 20 000 events/s quorum ack, 3 nodes on localhost Phase 10, Phase 14
M6 Append p99 latency (single node) < 5 ms single node, no replication Phase 2, Phase 14
M7 Append p99 latency (cluster) < 20 ms 3-node quorum, same rack Phase 10, Phase 14
M8 Append p99.9 latency (tail) < 50 ms single-node; < 100 ms cluster includes segment roll-over + index flush (§4.1) Phase 14 (Tail-Latency Analysis)
M9 Raft leader election latency ≤ 500 ms time from leader loss to new leader serving appends (§4.1) Phase 11
M10 Crash recovery time ≤ 60 s tail-scan + truncation + index rebuild, 10⁸ events (§4.1) Phase 9, Phase 14
M11 Cold startup time ≤ 30 s index rebuild from log scan if index missing, 10⁸ events (§4.1) Phase 9, Phase 14
M12 Global index memory ceiling ≤ 1 GB for 10⁸ events, ~10 bytes/entry (§4.1, C3) Phase 4

Benchmarking Strategy

  • Tool: criterion for micro/meso benchmarks; custom harness for macro throughput.
  • Benchmark targets per crate:
    • esdb-log: segment_append_1k, segment_read_sequential_1k, index_lookup_random_1k.
    • esdb-stream: append_single_stream_100, append_100_streams_interleaved, read_forward_1k.
    • esdb-subscribe: catchup_100k_from_zero, live_deliver_1k_events.
    • esdb-projection: rebuild_100k_events, incremental_10k_events.
    • esdb-replication: replicate_batch_100_3nodes.
  • Warm-up: 3 s; measurement: 10 s; 5 samples per benchmark.
  • Baseline stored in benches/baselines/; regression CI step diffs against baseline.

Profiling Methodology

  • CPU profiling: perf record -g (Linux) or Instruments (macOS); symbol resolution via perf report --stdio.
  • Flamegraphs: cargo flamegraph wrapping benchmark binary; output SVG committed to profiles/ for regressions.
  • Workflow: run benchmark → attach perf → generate flamegraph → identify hot path → patch → re-run → diff flamegraphs.
  • Hot-path targets by phase:
    • Phase 2/4: fsync dominates; measure syscall count per append batch via strace -e trace=fsync,pwrite64.
    • Phase 3: HashMap lock contention in version map; measure with parking_lot vs std::sync::RwLock under concurrent writers.
    • Phase 7: wakeup latency subscriber thread; measure via tokio-console task scheduling events.
    • Phase 10: Raft message serialization in hot path; measure prost encode/decode with criterion.

Allocator Analysis

  • Default: jemalloc via tikv-jemallocator in esdb-server for lower fragmentation under append workloads.
  • Heap profiling: jemalloc heap profiler (MALLOC_CONF=prof:true); dump on signal; analyze with jeprof.
  • Zero-copy path: esdb-log append path must not copy event Bytes; verified by allocator stats (alloc count per append must be O(1), not O(event_size)).
  • Arena allocation for ReplicationEntry batches: reuse allocation across append loop.

Lock Analysis

  • tokio-console for async task contention; identify tasks blocked on channel send/recv.
  • parking_lot's lock_api deadlock detector in debug builds.
  • Identify: per-stream RwLock in esdb-stream version map; global Mutex on commit_index in esdb-replication; both must show < 5% lock contention at target throughput.
  • Sharding: version map sharded by StreamId hash into 256 buckets to eliminate hot-lock bottleneck.

Cache Analysis

  • perf stat -e cache-misses,cache-references on segment read path.
  • Index layout: (stream, number) → offset index stored as sorted array; binary search; must fit L2 for hot streams (< 256 KB per stream index).
  • $all index: dense array of (GlobalPosition, file_offset) pairs; sequential scan must be cache-friendly (no pointer chasing).

Syscall Analysis

  • Target: ≤ 1 fsync per append batch (group-commit); verified with strace -c.
  • pwrite64 call count per append = 1 (length-prefix + payload in single write); verified.
  • io_uring considered for Phase 14 as optimization; baseline uses write + fsync.

Tail-Latency Analysis

  • HDR histogram (hdrhistogram crate) for append latency distribution; report p50, p95, p99, p99.9, max.
  • p99.9 target: < 50 ms single-node; < 100 ms cluster.
  • Identify GC-equivalent pauses: Rust has none, but tokio runtime scheduling jitter can cause tail spikes; measure with tokio::time::Instant around append future.
  • Jitter mitigation: pin append reactor thread to dedicated CPU core via core_affinity crate.

Memory Profiling

  • Baseline RSS at idle: < 50 MB for single-node esdb-server.
  • RSS under 1M events in-memory index: measure; must be < 500 MB (index is the dominant allocation).
  • Use valgrind massif for heap timeline during soak test; assert no unbounded growth.
  • Subscription buffer: bounded channel (capacity configurable, default 4 096 events); backpressure applied when full; no unbounded queue growth.

Scalability Analysis

Dominant Scaling Pressures

Pressure Driver Ceiling Mitigation
Global log size append volume × event size single-node disk capacity segment roll-over; scavenging Phase 9; tiered storage (future)
$all index memory one entry per committed event ~50 B/entry → 50 GB/10^9 events sparse index (every N-th entry + scan); mmap index file
Per-stream version map number of distinct streams ~100 B/stream → 10 GB/10^8 streams LRU eviction of cold streams; shard by hash
Raft replication throughput quorum round-trip latency × batch size ~20 000 events/s at 1 ms RTT batch pipelining; async AppendEntries; leader batching
Subscription fan-out concurrent subscribers × event rate N × event_rate memory/CPU push only to subscribed categories; backpressure
Projection rebuild time log size at rebuild trigger hours for 10^9 events snapshot checkpoints; parallel stream rebuild
Partition rebalancing stream count × partition move cost stall during ownership transfer consistent hashing; incremental rebalancing

Horizontal Scaling

  • Storage: does not scale horizontally within a single Raft group; scale by adding partitions (Phase 12). Each partition is an independent Raft group; total capacity = sum of per-partition capacities.
  • Read throughput: followers serve reads (with configurable staleness); add followers to scale read fan-out. Follower read scale-out is linear up to replication bandwidth limit.
  • Subscription/projection: stateless projection workers can scale horizontally if they share a checkpoint store; competing consumers (Phase 13) distribute load across consumer group.
  • Sharding: consistent hash ring (esdb-partition); default 16 virtual nodes per physical node; rebalancing moves ownership of virtual nodes.

Vertical Scaling

  • CPU: append path is single-threaded per stream (lock per stream); multiple streams scale linearly with cores up to I/O saturation.
  • RAM: primary limit is $all index and per-stream version map; larger RAM defers the need for sparse indexing.
  • Disk I/O: NVMe saturates at ~500 000 IOPS; group-commit batching reduces fsync to O(batches), not O(events); bottleneck shifts from IOPS to bandwidth at large event sizes.

Coordination Overhead

  • Raft adds one round-trip per append batch to the commit latency; pipeline depth (in-flight AppendEntries) configurable (default 16) to hide latency.
  • Leader election: O(N) messages for N nodes; adds up to election_timeout (150–300 ms) to append unavailability on leader failure.
  • Cross-partition $all merge: merge by GlobalPosition from P partitions costs O(P log P) per event delivered; with P ≤ 64 this is negligible.
  • Membership change (joint consensus): blocks new appends for one round-trip; frequency must be low (< 1/min).

Scaling Ceilings and Thresholds

Dimension Single-node ceiling 3-node cluster ceiling Notes
Events/s (append) ~50 000 ~20 000 (quorum) disk I/O and fsync bound
Total stored events ~10^9 (1 TB @ 1 KB/event) same per partition scavenge extends effective capacity
Concurrent streams ~10^7 (version map in RAM) same LRU eviction at threshold
Concurrent subscriptions ~10 000 ~10 000 per node fan-out CPU + memory bound
Projection read models ~1 000 ~1 000 checkpoint store I/O bound
Nodes per Raft group — ≤ 9 (odd; 5 typical) Raft coordination degrades > 9
Partitions N/A ≤ 1 024 cross-partition $all merge CPU

Storage Growth Model

  • Segment files: fixed 256 MB; roll at 90% full; scavenge triggered at configurable watermark (default: 70% deleted bytes in segment).
  • Index growth: sparse index with entry every 256 events; at 10^9 events → ~4 M index entries → ~32 MB (fits in RAM).
  • Snapshot store growth: one snapshot per aggregate per snapshot_interval events; bounded by number of active aggregates × snapshot_size.
  • Projection checkpoint store: one row per projection; O(projection count), not O(event count); negligible.

Scheduler and Thread Model Scaling

  • Tokio runtime: 1 thread per core for I/O; append path uses dedicated single-threaded runtime to avoid scheduling jitter.
  • Raft tick timer: 10 ms heartbeat interval; one async task per node; O(N) tasks for N-node cluster.
  • Subscription delivery: one async task per subscriber; at 10 000 subscribers → 10 000 tasks; within tokio's task limit but monitor RSS.
  • Backpressure propagation: bounded channels between log, replication, subscription, and projection layers; slow consumer slows only its own delivery path, not the append critical path.

Mental Models & First Principles

These distilled models are the conceptual load-bearing beams of the system. Each is stated as a compressed expert insight; violation of any one produces a predictable class of bugs.

MM1 — The Log Is the Database Insight: the append-only segment log is not a transport for a "real" database elsewhere; it is the source of truth. Every other data structure (stream-head index, snapshots, projections) is a disposable cache of the log. Prediction: any path that loses a committed log entry is a correctness bug, not a consistency bug. Bug class without it: engineers treat the projection state store as authoritative and skip log replay on recovery, silently dropping events. Applied in: §7 I1–I4, §6 P2, Phase 2, Phase 9.

MM2 — Optimistic Concurrency Is a Contract, Not a Hint Insight: ExpectedVersion is a compare-and-swap published in the API contract. The server must atomically compare and append under the stream lock; relaxing this to "usually check, then write" creates a TOCTOU race that violates the DDD aggregate consistency boundary. Prediction: under concurrent writers the only serialisation errors should be WrongExpectedVersion; spurious successes are impossible if the CAS is atomic. Bug class without it: two concurrent writers both read version N, both pass the check, both write EventNumber N+1 — stream history branches. Applied in: §7 I5, Phase 3, ADR-13.

MM3 — Global Position Is a Clock, Not a Key Insight: GlobalPosition is a strictly monotone counter that acts as a logical clock for the whole cluster. Subscribers use it as a cursor; ordering across streams is established only through this counter. Prediction: any gap in GlobalPosition values signals a lost commit; any non-monotone position signals a replication safety violation. Bug class without it: read-model builders interleave events from different streams using wall-clock timestamps, producing non-deterministic projections. Applied in: §7 I3, Phase 4, Phase 7, ADR-23.

MM4 — fsync Before Ack Is Non-Negotiable Insight: an append that has been acknowledged to the client must survive a subsequent process crash. This requires fsync/fdatasync to complete before the ack is returned, not after. Group-commit amortizes the cost across a batch; the invariant cannot be traded away for throughput. Prediction: removing the pre-ack fsync converts a durable store into a lossy buffer; RPO becomes nonzero. Bug class without it: power-cycle under load loses the tail of the log silently; crash recovery finds a truncated segment with no evidence of the lost writes. Applied in: §7 I4, §4.2 latency budget, Phase 2, ADR-09.

MM5 — Raft Safety Requires Log Matching, Not Just Quorum Insight: Raft's quorum commit alone is insufficient; the Log Matching Property (if two logs have an entry with the same index and term, all preceding entries are identical) is what guarantees that a newly elected leader does not overwrite committed entries. Any deviation from Raft's AppendEntries consistency check breaks this. Prediction: a follower that accepts out-of-order entries will diverge silently; the cluster appears healthy until a leader election exposes the inconsistency. Bug class without it: follower accepts batch at log_index=N without verifying prev_log_index/prev_log_term; after election, new leader's log conflicts with durable committed entries. Applied in: §7 I7, Phase 10, Phase 11, ADR-11.

MM6 — The Catch-Up/Live Boundary Is a Safety Transition, Not a Convenience Switch Insight: the transition from catch-up replay (sequential scan) to live delivery (push from in-memory tail) must be gapless and overlap-free. The transition buffer (live-latch) must hold in-flight events during the handoff; neither dropping nor duplicating events is acceptable at this boundary. Prediction: a subscriber that misses the transition window sees a gap; one that does not drain the buffer sees duplicates. Idempotency alone does not cover gaps. Bug class without it: projection built on top of a gapped subscription computes a wrong aggregate count permanently, silently. Applied in: §7 I3, Phase 7, ADR-03.

MM7 — Snapshots Are Caches With Schema Versions Insight: a snapshot is a compressed replay of events up to a version; it is valid only if the projection's schema_version matches the snapshot's recorded schema_version. An incompatible snapshot loaded as if it were current silently corrupts the aggregate's in-memory state. Prediction: changing a projection's state shape without invalidating snapshots causes silent wrong reads until a full replay is forced. Bug class without it: after a schema migration, stale snapshots are loaded for existing aggregates; new events are folded onto wrong base state. Applied in: §7 I9, Phase 6, ADR-14.

MM8 — Scavenge Must Preserve Event Numbers and Positions Insight: scavenge rewrites segments to remove deleted events but must preserve the EventNumber and GlobalPosition of all surviving events unchanged. Subscribers and cursors are keyed to these values; renumbering surviving events after scavenge would invalidate all active cursors. Prediction: scavenge that renumbers events invalidates the O(10^4) active cursor checkpoints stored in subscriber state stores. Bug class without it: post-scavenge read returns event at position N where N was previously a deleted event; cursor-advancing logic skips or replays an event. Applied in: §7 I10, Phase 9, ADR-30.

MM9 — Determinism Is the Foundation of Exactly-Once Projection Insight: a projection achieves exactly-once semantics through idempotent deterministic handlers plus checkpointed cursors, not through deduplication locks. If the handler function produces the same output for the same input every time, replaying from checkpoint is safe. Any external side-effect or non-deterministic state in the handler breaks this contract. Prediction: a projection handler that generates a UUID on each call will produce different output on replay, corrupting the read model. Bug class without it: after a projection worker crash, checkpoint rollback triggers replay of the last N events; non-idempotent handler double-writes records. Applied in: §7 I8, Phase 8, §6 P9.

MM10 — Consensus Does Not Substitute for Idempotency at the Application Layer Insight: Raft guarantees that a committed entry is not lost; it does not prevent a client from retrying an append after a timeout (before it knows whether the previous attempt was committed). The server must therefore honour EventId-based deduplication at the storage layer to make client retries safe. Prediction: removing EventId dedup converts a retry into a duplicate append that violates the client's intent. Bug class without it: a network timeout causes a client to retry an AppendEvents call; the server appends the event twice; a projection counting events over-counts. Applied in: §7 I6, Phase 1, Phase 3, ADR-17.

Comparative Architecture Table

The table below compares this blueprint's design against four production event-store and streaming systems along the dimensions most relevant to the build decisions made here.

Dimension This Blueprint EventStoreDB 23+ Apache Kafka 3.x Datomic (Cloud) Axon Server 4.x
Storage model Append-only per-stream segment log; global log via commit positions Per-stream append-only chunk files; global $all log Partition-local append-only segment log; no per-stream index Immutable datom store (B-tree of EAVt facts); separate storage node In-memory first; journal to disk; no native segment log
Ordering guarantee Total order within stream (EventNumber); per-partition global order (GlobalPosition) Total order within stream; global $all total order (single node or cluster) Total order within partition only; no cross-partition ordering Transaction log total order; basis (t-value) as logical clock Total order per aggregate; global sequence for all events on single node
Optimistic concurrency ExpectedVersion enum (Any/NoStream/StreamExists/Exact); atomic CAS under per-stream lock Same ExpectedVersion enum (copied by community); same CAS semantics No native OCC; applications use consumer-group offsets or external CAS Transaction-time CAS via :db/cas attribute; native to the transactor ExpectedSequenceNumber on command; enforced by aggregate command handler
Replication Raft (multi-node log replication); quorum commit; pipeline AppendEntries Leader/follower gossip with internal cluster bus; quorum-acknowledged writes Raft (KRaft mode); partition-level leader election; ISR quorum Dynamo-style replication via cloud storage (S3/DynamoDB backing) JVM-based gossip + Axon Server HA via leader/follower; not Raft
Durability model fsync before ack; CRC32C per entry; torn-write detection on recovery fsync before ack (configurable); CRC checksums; chunk file recovery scan acks=all + min.insync.replicas; fsync configurable per broker Cloud storage durability SLA; transactor is stateless; no local fsync Configurable; default async; fsync optional
Projection / read-model Pluggable projection engine with checkpoint store; catch-up + live subscription Built-in persistent projections (JS); catch-up and persistent subscriptions Kafka Streams / ksqlDB (separate process); consumer-offset checkpointing Datalog queries over immutable history; no separate projection worker Upcaster + event handler pattern; built-in TrackingEventProcessor
Compaction / TTL Online scavenge (Phase 9); RoaringBitmap live-range; preserves EventNumber Scavenge operation; hard-delete or soft-delete with $maxAge/$maxCount Log compaction (key-based tombstone); time-based TTL via retention.ms No compaction; full history is a first-class feature; excise for GDPR No native compaction; disk pressure managed by archiving
Wire protocol gRPC + protobuf (ADR-02) TCP + custom ESDB binary protocol; gRPC added in v21 Binary Kafka protocol over TCP; no gRPC Transit+JSON or AWS SDK; proprietary API gRPC via Axon Server Connector
Partitioning / sharding Consistent hash ring (Phase 12); per-partition Raft group Cluster topology with stream affinity; not consistent-hash Configurable partition count; producer key-based routing; rebalancing Storage-level sharding via cloud backing store Single-node or multi-context HA; not natively sharded
Language / runtime Rust (no GC, predictable tail latency) .NET / C# (GC; configurable GC mode) Java / JVM (G1GC; GC pause at scale is a known concern) Clojure / JVM Java / JVM

EventStoreDB is the closest spiritual ancestor: it invented the ExpectedVersion API and the per-stream/global-log duality that this blueprint implements. This blueprint diverges by using Raft (instead of ESDB's proprietary cluster bus), Rust (instead of .NET), and a hand-built segment log (instead of ESDB's chunk files). The result is a lower-level, more pedagogically transparent system that trades operational simplicity for implementation insight.

Apache Kafka solves a different problem: it is a high-throughput partitioned log for event streaming, not an event store for aggregate-centric writes. Kafka has no per-stream ExpectedVersion, no native OCC, and no built-in event schema-version negotiation. Its partition-level ordering is weaker than this system's per-stream EventNumber semantics. This blueprint borrows Kafka's segment-roll and index ideas (P5, R2) but rejects its consumer-group model for aggregate writes.

Datomic inverts the model: rather than per-stream typed events, it stores facts (datoms) in a universal EAVt schema with full audit history. Its query model (Datalog) is far more expressive than this system's projection engine. Datomic's transactor is single-writer by design, matching this blueprint's stream-level serialisation, but Datomic makes horizontal write scaling explicit only through partition-level sharding in the backing cloud store. This blueprint rejects Datomic's schema-first approach in favour of opaque event payloads that preserve domain encapsulation.

Axon Server sits at the application-framework end of the spectrum: it is a JVM-based event store tightly coupled to the Axon Framework's CQRS abstractions. Its TrackingEventProcessor and competing-consumer patterns (Phase 13) are analogues to this blueprint's persistent subscriptions, but Axon Server's replication model is simpler (leader/follower, not Raft) and its durability guarantees are weaker by default. This blueprint is inspired by Axon's projection checkpoint model (R8) but targets a lower-level API.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All numbers are derived step-by-step from the §4 System Constraints table. No number is asserted without derivation.

Input parameters (from §4.1):

  • Target sustained append rate (cluster leader): W = 10,000 events/s
  • Target sustained read/catch-up rate: R = 200,000 events/s
  • Average event payload: P = 512 bytes (typical domain event; §4 max = 4 MB, typical << 64 KB)
  • Event record wire overhead: H = 64 bytes (§4.4)
  • Global index entry size (position → offset): G = 12 bytes (§4.4)
  • Stream index entry size: S_entry = 16 bytes (§4.4)
  • Memory per idle stream: M_stream = 256 bytes (§4.1)
  • Segment file size: SEG = 256 MB (§4.1 default)
  • Scavenge I/O budget: ≤ 20% of append bandwidth (§4.1)

C1 — Raw write bandwidth (single node, no replication)

Bytes per event = H + P = 64 + 512 = 576 bytes
Sustained write bandwidth = W × 576 = 10,000 × 576 = 5.76 MB/s

At NVMe sequential write ≥ 500 MB/s (§4.1 recommendation): 5.76 MB/s uses ~1.15% of disk bandwidth. The disk is not the bottleneck at this event rate; fsync batching (group-commit window = 1 ms) dominates latency, not throughput.

C2 — Segment roll frequency

Time to fill one 256 MB segment = SEG / write_bandwidth
  = 256 MB / 5.76 MB/s ≈ 44.4 seconds

Segments roll approximately every 44 seconds at the sustained target rate. At peak double-rate (20,000 events/s): ~22 seconds. Segment roll-over adds at most one extra fsync (§4.1 p99.9 ≤ 50 ms accounts for this).

C3 — Global index RAM at 10^8 events

Index entries = 10^8
Memory = 10^8 × G = 10^8 × 12 bytes = 1,200 MB ≈ 1.2 GB

§4.1 target: ≤ 1 GB for 10^8 events at 10 bytes/entry. At 12 bytes/entry (§4.4 exact figure) this is 1.2 GB, slightly over the 1 GB target. The discrepancy is resolved by the sparse-index mitigation: one entry per 256 events reduces to 4.7 MB — well within budget.

C4 — Stream head index RAM at 10^7 streams

Memory = 10^7 streams × M_stream = 10^7 × 256 bytes = 2,560 MB = 2.5 GB

§4.1 target: ≤ 500 MB for 10^7 streams at ~50 bytes/stream. The §4.4 definition uses 16 bytes/entry (index entry only). The 256 bytes/stream in §4.1 covers the full in-memory stream-head record (version + segment pointer + dedup ring slice). At 10^7 streams, LRU eviction of cold streams to disk is required to meet the 500 MB target; active cold-stream eviction threshold ≈ 10^7 × 50 bytes = 500 MB.

C5 — Daily storage growth

Daily events = W × 86,400 s = 10,000 × 86,400 = 8.64 × 10^8 events/day
Daily bytes   = 8.64 × 10^8 × 576 bytes ≈ 497 GB/day ≈ 0.5 TB/day

At §4.1 storage amplification ≤ 1.05×: actual disk consumed ≤ 0.5 TB × 1.05 = 525 GB/day. At target retention of 30 days (configurable): ~15.75 TB of raw disk required before scavenging. Scavenge at 70% deleted-byte watermark can reclaim up to 70% of segments where events are tombstoned.

C6 — Scavenge I/O budget

Scavenge read+write bandwidth = 20% of append bandwidth
  = 20% × 5.76 MB/s = 1.15 MB/s
Segments scavenged per second = 1.15 MB/s / (2 × 256 MB) ≈ 1 segment per 445 seconds

One segment takes approximately 7.4 minutes to scavenge at the I/O-budget-limited rate. At daily roll rate of ~1,964 segments/day, the scavenger must process 1,964 segments/day = ~1.4 segments/minute to keep up. At the budget cap (1 segment per 7.4 minutes), scavenge lags production rate by ~10×. The mitigation is to widen the scavenge I/O budget during off-peak hours or to lower the 70% trigger watermark to reduce scavengeable segments.

C7 — Raft replication bandwidth (3-node cluster)

Replication bandwidth per follower = W × 576 bytes = 5.76 MB/s
Total replication bandwidth (leader → 2 followers) = 2 × 5.76 = 11.52 MB/s

On a 1 Gbps LAN (125 MB/s): replication uses ~9.2% of available bandwidth. At 10× burst (100,000 events/s): 115 MB/s — near saturation. NIC bonding or 10 Gbps interconnect is required above ~130,000 events/s.

C8 — Election timeout impact on availability

Leader failure window = election_timeout_max + quorum_ack_time
  = 300 ms + 500 µs ≈ 300.5 ms

Per §4.1 RTO ≤ 2 min (node failure): election adds ≤ 300 ms unavailability, well within RTO. At 10,000 events/s: events lost during election window (in-flight, not yet committed) ≈ 10,000 × 0.3 = 3,000 events that must be retried by clients (RPO = 0 for committed events per §4.1).

C9 — Subscription fan-out memory

Memory per active subscription = 64 KB (§4.1)
At 10,000 concurrent subscribers: 10,000 × 64 KB = 640 MB

This is a significant allocation. Per §4.1 target of 10,000 concurrent subscriptions per node, the subscription layer alone consumes 640 MB RAM. Combined with stream-head index (500 MB) and global index (sparse: 4.7 MB), total process RSS target ≈ ~1.5–2 GB for the steady-state server process.

C10 — Projection catch-up time for 10^8 events

Catch-up rate = 100,000 events/s (§4.1)
Time to replay 10^8 events = 10^8 / 100,000 = 1,000 s ≈ 16.7 minutes

A cold-start projection rebuild from 10^8 events takes ~17 minutes without snapshots. With snapshots at interval I = 1,000 events: worst-case tail replay = 1,000 events = 10 ms — negligible. Snapshot storage cost per aggregate: one snapshot per 1,000 events × (application-defined snapshot size ≤ 1 MB) = bounded by active-aggregate count.

C11 — Index rebuild on crash recovery (10^8 events)

Sequential scan bandwidth = 500 MB/s (NVMe, §4.1)
Total log size for 10^8 events = 10^8 × 576 bytes = 57.6 GB
Scan time = 57.6 GB / 500 MB/s = 115.2 s ≈ 1.9 minutes

§4.1 cold startup target ≤ 30 s for 10^8 events. A raw sequential scan at 500 MB/s takes 115 s, which exceeds the 30 s target. The resolution is the persisted index file: if the index file is valid (verified by tail-segment CRC check), startup uses the file directly, taking ≤ 5 s (§4.1 warm startup). The 30 s cold target therefore requires that the index file covers all but the last segment's tail (the tail-scan covers the remainder in ≤ 256 MB / 500 MB/s = 0.5 s).

C12 — Cross-partition $all merge overhead (P partitions)

Merge cost per delivered event = O(P log P), P = number of partitions
At P = 64 partitions, P log₂ P = 64 × 6 = 384 comparisons per event
At R = 200,000 events/s: 200,000 × 384 = 76.8 × 10^6 comparisons/s
At ~10^9 comparisons/s per core: 76.8 × 10^6 / 10^9 ≈ 0.077 cores

Cross-partition merge at 64 partitions and 200,000 events/s consumes < 0.1 CPU cores — negligible. The §4.1 cap of ≤ 1,024 partitions at 200,000 events/s: 1,024 × 10 × 200,000 / 10^9 ≈ 2 cores — still acceptable on an 8-core server.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

These questions require synthesis across two or more sections. They are the questions a staff-level reviewer would ask during a design review or on-call handoff.

HQ1 — If a leader crashes immediately after writing to its own log but before sending AppendEntries to followers, what happens to that entry, and where in the codebase is the resolution handled? The entry is uncommitted (commit_index has not advanced); after election, the new leader will have a higher term. The Log Matching Property (MM5, Phase 10, I7) ensures the new leader will overwrite this entry if its own log is authoritative. The resolution is in Phase 10's RaftLog::reconcile_with_leader() — followers truncate divergent tail entries on receiving AppendEntries with a conflicting prev_log_term.

HQ2 — Can a subscription deliver the same event twice without a client crash? Under what conditions, and which invariant governs this? Yes, during the catch-up/live transition (Phase 7) if the live-latch buffer is not drained before the subscriber's catch-up cursor reaches the transition point. This is governed by I3 (commit positions strictly increasing) and the live-latch design (Phase 7.2). The subscriber's idempotent EventId dedup (I6, Phase 1) is the client-side defence; the server cannot prevent the overlap without a per-subscriber ack protocol.

HQ3 — The §4.1 table claims append p99 ≤ 10 ms. The §4.2 latency budget sums to ~900 µs for p50. What accounts for the 10× gap to p99, and which phases are responsible for the tail? The p99 tail is dominated by fsync jitter (group-commit window ≤ 1 ms, but OS flush scheduling adds variance), segment roll-over (Phase 2, Phase 9), and lock contention under concurrent writers on the same stream (Phase 3, ADR-13). The 50 ms p99.9 includes segment roll-over (new segment creation + index flush). ADR-09 (custom segment format) and ADR-13 (per-stream lock) are the primary ADR decisions that constrain this budget.

HQ4 — If the scavenge operation (Phase 9) is interrupted mid-rewrite, what prevents data loss or a corrupted segment from being served to readers? Scavenge writes to a new segment file atomically renamed over the old one only after the new file is fully written and fsynced (Phase 9, I10, ADR-30). The RoaringBitmap live-range (Phase 9.2) is computed before the rewrite begins; any event present in the live-range bitmap is written to the new segment. An interrupted rewrite leaves the old segment intact (the rename never executes); on restart, the scavenge resumes from the last committed segment boundary.

HQ5 — How does the system ensure that a snapshot loaded for aggregate reconstruction is not stale with respect to the current projection schema? What happens if a stale snapshot is loaded? Snapshots carry a schema_version field (Phase 6, I9, ADR-14). On load, the aggregate loader compares snapshot.schema_version against the projection's compiled SCHEMA_VERSION constant. A mismatch causes the snapshot to be discarded and a full tail-replay to be performed from EventNumber(0). Without this check, folding new events onto a snapshot built from an incompatible state shape produces silently wrong aggregate state (MM7).

HQ6 — The capacity model (C4) shows the stream-head index at 2.5 GB for 10^7 streams but §4.1 targets ≤ 500 MB. What is the reconciliation mechanism and where is it implemented? LRU eviction of cold stream heads to disk (Phase 5, §4.1 mitigation column). The in-memory stream-head map evicts the least-recently-used entries when RSS exceeds the configured threshold. Evicted entries are serialised to the stream index file (Phase 5.2). On next access, the entry is promoted back to the in-memory map. This means a cold-stream read incurs one additional random disk seek (≤ 500 µs per §4.1 stream read p50 latency). See also C4 derivation in the Capacity Model.

HQ7 — What prevents two Raft leaders from simultaneously accepting writes (split-brain) after a network partition? Raft's quorum requirement (I7, Phase 11): a leader can only commit an entry if a majority of nodes (≥ 2 of 3) acknowledge it. During a network partition, the minority partition cannot form a quorum; its stale leader cannot advance commit_index. The majority partition elects a new leader with a higher term; the old leader's uncommitted entries are overwritten via the Log Matching Property (Phase 10, ADR-11). Leader leases (ADR-22) must be bounded by the election timeout to prevent stale leaders from serving reads after partition.

HQ8 — If a projection worker crashes after writing a read-model record but before checkpointing its cursor, what happens on restart, and which invariant makes this safe? On restart the worker loads the last committed checkpoint (Phase 8, I8). It replays from the checkpointed position, re-processing the already-applied events. I8 (deterministic projection replay) and I6 (EventId idempotency key) guarantee that re-processing the same events produces the same output. If the projection handler is side-effect-free with respect to external systems (§6 P9), the replay is safe. MM9 identifies non-deterministic handler state as the primary violation risk.

HQ9 — How does the consistent-hash ring in Phase 12 handle stream routing after a node leaves the cluster? What is the worst-case window of unavailability for streams owned by the departing node? On node departure, the virtual nodes assigned to it are redistributed to the remaining physical nodes (Phase 12, ADR-25). During redistribution, streams owned by the departing node's virtual nodes are unavailable until ownership transfer is complete (Phase 12.2 incremental rebalancing). Worst-case unavailability = time to elect a new Raft leader for the migrated partition + log catch-up time ≤ RTO = 2 min (§4.1). Active clients receive a PartitionMoved gRPC error and retry routing.

HQ10 — Can the global index become inconsistent with the segment log? Under what failure scenario, and how is it detected and repaired? Yes: if the process crashes after writing a segment entry but before updating the in-memory global index, the index will be behind the log. Recovery detects this via the tail-scan in Phase 9 (crash recovery, §4.1 crash recovery time ≤ 60 s): the recovery scanner reads the segment tail from the last known index entry, reconstructs missing index entries, and truncates any entry whose CRC32C is invalid (P3, Phase 2, ADR-09). The global index file is then rewritten from the in-memory reconstruction before the server opens for writes.

HQ11 — The system targets RPO = 0 for quorum-committed events. What is the RPO for an event that has been acked to the client but not yet replicated to a quorum follower? RPO = 0 applies only to quorum-committed events (§4.1: "Committed = quorum-durable"). An ack is sent to the client only after quorum commit (Phase 10, I7, ADR-11). Therefore there is no window where a client-acknowledged event is not quorum-durable. The single-node configuration (no Raft) has a different contract: ack-after-fsync only, RPO = 0 for single-node failure but positive RPO for total hardware failure.

HQ12 — What is the space complexity of the dedup ring per stream, and when does it fail to prevent duplicates? The dedup ring (Phase 1, I6, ADR-17) is a fixed-size circular buffer of recently-seen EventIds per stream. Its space is O(ring_size × UUID_size) = O(ring_size × 16 bytes). It fails to prevent duplicates when the retry interval exceeds the ring's time window (ring wraps before the duplicate arrives) — typically when a client retries after a delay longer than (ring_size / write_rate_per_stream). For a 1,000-entry ring and 100 writes/s per stream, the window is 10 s. Retries beyond 10 s after the original write may succeed as duplicates; clients are responsible for not retrying beyond this window or for using application-level idempotency.

HQ13 — Phase 4 assigns GlobalPosition values. Who assigns them in a multi-partition cluster, and how does this interact with the Raft leader? In a single-Raft-group deployment (Phases 1–11), the Raft leader assigns GlobalPosition as entries are committed to the Raft log (Phase 4, Phase 10, ADR-23). In a multi-partition cluster (Phase 12), each partition has its own independent position space; there is no globally unique position across partitions. The cross-partition $all subscription merges per-partition positions using a vector-clock-like tuple (Phase 12.2, C12). Clients that require strict cross-partition total order must use an external sequencer not in scope for this blueprint.

HQ14 — What distinguishes a "persistent subscription" from a "catch-up subscription" in this blueprint, and what additional failure mode does the persistent variant introduce? A catch-up subscription is stateless from the server's perspective: the client provides its starting position and the server streams from there (Phase 7). A persistent (competing-consumer) subscription has server-side state: the server manages the group's cursor, ack state, and message assignment among competing consumers (Phase 13). The additional failure mode is ack-loss: if a consumer crashes after processing but before acking, the event is re-delivered to another consumer in the group. This requires consumer-side idempotency (I6, I8) and a server-side nack/park mechanism (Phase 13.2).

HQ15 — The §5 Required Reading includes Ongaro's Raft paper (P1) and the Fisher-Lynch-Paterson impossibility result (via B1). How does the FLP impossibility constrain the election timeout design, and where is this reflected in the blueprint? FLP proves that in an asynchronous system with even one faulty process, no deterministic consensus algorithm can guarantee termination. Raft's resolution is randomised election timeouts (150–300 ms, §4.1): randomisation makes the probability of livelock arbitrarily small but does not eliminate it theoretically. The blueprint reflects this in Phase 11 (election timeout range chosen per Ongaro sec. 9.3 practical guidance) and ADR-11 (the 150–300 ms range is explicitly justified by Raft's empirical convergence, not by a theoretical bound).

HQ16 — If the segment index file is present but its last entry does not match the last entry in the segment log's tail, which takes precedence and why? The segment log (WAL) is the source of truth per MM1 and I1. The index file is a cache. On startup, the recovery scanner reads the segment tail from the index's last recorded offset and verifies each subsequent entry's CRC32C (Phase 2, Phase 9 crash recovery, ADR-09). Valid tail entries are added to the index; invalid entries cause truncation at the last valid entry. The index is then persisted. This means the index file's stale/corrupted tail is always overwritten by the ground truth in the segment log.
