HQ17 — The blueprint targets 99.9% availability. What is the maximum permissible downtime per month, and does the RTO budget support it? 99.9% availability = 0.1% downtime = 43.8 minutes/month. RTO ≤ 2 min per node failure (§4.1). If failure frequency ≤ 21 failures/month ≈ 1 failure every 34 hours, the availability target is met. In practice, planned maintenance windows (rolling restarts during Phase 13 rolling upgrade) add to this budget; each node restart = 1 leader election (≤ 300 ms) + follower catch-up (bounded by RTO). The budget is tight for frequent deployments; blue/green partition rolling (Phase 14) is recommended to avoid consecutive elections.

HQ18 — Can two writers to the same stream both receive a success ack? Under what conditions would this be a correctness violation vs. expected behaviour? Yes — if both use ExpectedVersion::Any, both writes succeed regardless of the stream's current version. This is expected behaviour: Any explicitly opts out of OCC. It is a correctness violation only if the application's domain invariant requires a single-writer contract (e.g., aggregate state mutation). The distinction is documented in Phase 3 (I5, ADR-13): ExpectedVersion::Exact(N) enforces single-writer; Any is for append-only event logs where ordering doesn't matter for a given logical entity.

HQ19 — How does the blueprint handle backward-incompatible changes to the protobuf event schema across a rolling cluster upgrade? ADR-08 (semantic versioning for wire format) and Phase 13.2 (rolling upgrade protocol): protobuf wire-format compatibility requires that new fields use new field numbers (never reuse), old fields are never removed (only deprecated), and the schema_version field on AppendRequest gates deserialization behavior. During a rolling upgrade, nodes running old and new versions coexist; the leader must accept writes from clients using either schema version. Phase 14 (compatibility testing) validates this with a multi-version integration test harness.

HQ20 — The scavenge operation uses an atomic rename to replace a segment. What happens to readers that have an open mmap handle to the old segment file at the moment of rename? On Linux, rename(2) is atomic at the filesystem level; existing open file handles to the old inode remain valid until the last close (POSIX inode reference semantics). Readers with open mmap handles continue to access the old inode's data; no data is lost. After all readers close their handles, the old inode is reclaimed. This is the deliberate design in Phase 9 (ADR-30): the atomic rename + inode persistence makes scavenge transparent to concurrent readers without a read-lock on the segment.

HQ21 — What is the mechanism by which a newly elected leader discovers the GlobalPosition of the last committed event, and how long does this take? On election, the new leader reads its own Raft log to find the highest log_index with commit_index set by the previous leader (Phase 10, Phase 11, I7). The GlobalPosition is embedded in each Raft log entry (Phase 10.2). Discovery is O(1) — the leader reads commit_index from its persistent Raft state, then looks up the corresponding log entry. Total time: one disk seek to the Raft log index + one entry decode ≈ 500 µs (§4.1 stream read p50). The leader does not need to contact followers before advertising its GlobalPosition.

HQ22 — Why does the blueprint choose per-stream striped locks (ADR-13) over a single global lock or a lock-free approach? A single global lock serialises all stream writes, capping throughput at one writer regardless of CPU count — it would cap at far below the 20,000 events/s target (§4.1). Lock-free CAS (e.g., atomic compare-and-swap on a shared GlobalPosition counter) is feasible but requires careful ABA-protection and cannot easily enforce the full ExpectedVersion enum semantics (particularly NoStream and StreamExists checks). Per-stream striped locks (one RwLock per stripe bucket) allow O(cores) parallel writes across different streams while preserving the atomic CAS contract per stream. ADR-13 documents the lock-free alternative as the "When the Rejected Choice Wins" case for extreme write fan-out where per-stream lock overhead dominates.

HQ23 — How does the system ensure that a subscription does not consume unbounded memory if the subscriber is slow? Bounded channels between the log layer and each subscriber's delivery task (Phase 7, ADR-03). When the channel fills, the sender (log tail push) applies backpressure: it blocks on send rather than buffering. Each subscriber's channel is bounded at a configurable size (default: matches the 64 KB ack-window per §4.1). A subscriber that falls far behind triggers flow-control on the subscription worker, not on the append critical path. The subscriber receives a SlowConsumerWarning notification (Phase 13) and may be disconnected at the operator's configured lag threshold.

HQ24 — The blueprint mentions io_uring as optional. What is the specific performance advantage of io_uring for this workload, and why is it not the primary path? io_uring (Linux 5.1+) enables submission-queue-based async I/O that avoids per-syscall context switches for high-IOPS workloads. For the sequential-append workload (Phase 2), the primary bottleneck is fsync latency, not syscall overhead; io_uring's advantage is marginal for sequential writes. For random-read catch-up (Phase 5), io_uring can batch multiple segment reads and reduce latency at high concurrency. It is not the primary path because: (1) it requires Linux ≥ 5.1, reducing portability (§4.1 OS target: Linux primary, macOS dev); (2) the tokio-uring crate is not yet stable enough for production use (ADR-03).

HQ25 — If the blueprint's consistent-hash ring assigns stream S to partition P, but a client routes to partition Q (due to a stale ring view), what happens? The receiving node Q detects the ownership mismatch by checking its local ring state (Phase 12.2). It returns a PartitionMoved { correct_partition: P } gRPC error. The client's routing layer refreshes its ring view from the cluster metadata service and retries on partition P. The stale-ring window is bounded by the ring gossip propagation delay (Phase 12, gossip interval configurable). No data is written to the wrong partition; writes are always rejected on routing mismatch, never silently rerouted.

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Alt-1 — Single-Node Relational Event Store (PostgreSQL + Append-Only Table)

  Client
    |
    v
 PostgreSQL
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  events(stream_id, event_number, global_seq, data)  │
  │  index on (stream_id, event_number)                 │
  │  index on global_seq                               │
  │  SERIAL global_seq (or sequences)                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Projections: polling SELECT WHERE global_seq > $cursor
  OCC: UPDATE streams SET version=N+1 WHERE version=N

Regime where it wins: teams with < 5,000 events/s, existing PostgreSQL operational expertise, no dedicated storage budget, and a requirement for ad-hoc SQL queries over event history. ACID transactions allow transactional outbox patterns without a separate inbox table. Schema migrations are familiar. Operational tooling (pg_dump, point-in-time recovery) is mature.

Real system embodying it: Marten (https://martendb.io/) — a .NET event store and document database built on top of PostgreSQL. Also used by many teams rolling their own events table.

Why this blueprint rejected it: PostgreSQL's WAL is not optimised for append-only workloads at high event rates. At ≥ 20,000 events/s, table bloat, VACUUM pressure, and index maintenance overhead become dominant concerns. The global sequence via SERIAL or sequences is a single serialisation point that caps append throughput. Sequential scan of an events table is far slower than direct segment-file scan at 500 MB/s (C11). Most critically, this architecture cannot achieve the §4.1 throughput targets without significant PostgreSQL tuning that brings it back toward the custom storage approach.

Alt-2 — Kafka-Backed Event Store (Kafka as Storage + Consumer Groups as Subscriptions)

  Client
    |
    v
  Event Store Service (thin wrapper)
    |         |
    v         v
  Kafka     State Store
  Topics    (RocksDB or Redis)
  ┌──────┐  ┌──────────────┐
  │stream│  │stream_versions│
  │ per  │  │(OCC checks)  │
  │topic │  └──────────────┘
  └──────┘
  Projections: Kafka Streams / consumer groups
  OCC: External lock service or optimistic CAS in state store

Regime where it wins: organisations already operating Kafka at scale, with existing Kafka expertise and tooling investment. Kafka's consumer group model provides competing-consumer subscriptions out of the box. At very high throughput (> 500,000 events/s), Kafka's partition-level write path (no per-stream lock) outperforms a per-stream-locked event store. Kafka's log.retention.ms and log.compaction provide time-based and key-based retention without custom scavenge logic.

Real system embodying it: several production DDD/CQRS implementations use Kafka as the underlying storage with a thin service layer for OCC and stream-level ordering. Confluent's "Event Sourcing with Kafka" reference architecture is a documented example.

Why this blueprint rejected it: Kafka has no native per-stream ExpectedVersion; implementing OCC requires an external state store (RocksDB, Redis, or PostgreSQL), introducing a second system and a new failure mode. Kafka's cross-partition ordering guarantees are weaker: there is no global position across topics/partitions without an external sequencer. Event replay for aggregate loading requires reading a full topic partition rather than a stream slice, which is expensive for streams with many events spread across a large partition. Kafka's operational model (Zookeeper/KRaft, broker configuration, ISR management) is significantly more complex than a purpose-built event store for teams that do not already operate Kafka.

Alt-3 — LSMT-Based Event Store (RocksDB as Storage Engine)

  Client
    |
    v
  Event Store Service
    |
    v
  RocksDB (embedded)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CF: events   key=(stream_id, event_number)  │
  │  CF: global   key=global_seq                 │
  │  CF: meta     key=stream_id, value=head_ver  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Compaction: RocksDB native LSM compaction
  Replication: RocksDB + Raft via Tikv-style replication

Regime where it wins: workloads with high point-lookup rates (random reads by stream_id + event_number dominate over sequential scan), large numbers of small streams (RocksDB's bloom filters amortise point lookup cost), and teams that want to leverage RocksDB's battle-tested compaction logic rather than writing a custom scavenge. Also wins when the event payload is small (< 128 bytes) and the overhead of a custom framed log would be disproportionate.

Real system embodying it: TiKV (Rust + RocksDB + Raft) uses this architecture for general-purpose transactional key-value storage. CockroachDB uses a similar pattern (Pebble, a Go RocksDB port, as storage engine).

Why this blueprint rejected it: RocksDB's LSM compaction is not aware of event-sourcing access patterns. Sequential segment scan (the dominant access pattern for catch-up subscriptions) is faster on a custom append-only log than on RocksDB's SST files, which are optimised for random access. RocksDB's write amplification (typically 10–30×) is a poor fit for an append-only workload where the write amplification target is ≤ 1.05× (§4.1 storage amplification). Embedding RocksDB also couples the storage engine's version and build system to this project; the custom segment log (Phase 2) provides full control over the storage format and crash-recovery semantics without a dependency on a large C++ library (ADR-09).

Alt-4 — Immutable Ledger with Merkle-Chained Segments (Blockchain-Adjacent)

  Client
    |
    v
  Append Service
    |
    v
  Segment Chain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Segment N:  hash(events) + prev_segment_hash       │
  │  Segment N+1: hash(events) + hash(Segment N header) │
  │  ...                                                │
  │  Root hash published to external notarisation log   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Audit: any client can verify chain integrity from genesis

Regime where it wins: regulated industries (finance, healthcare, supply chain) where tamper-evidence of the event log must be provable to a third party without trusting the operator. The Merkle chain allows an auditor to verify that a specific event was present in the log at a specific time using only the root hash and an inclusion proof, without downloading the full log. Also wins when the threat model includes a malicious administrator.

Real system embodying it: Amazon QLDB (Quantum Ledger Database) uses a cryptographically verifiable journal with a Merkle tree over all committed transactions. Hyperledger Fabric uses a similar ledger model for permissioned blockchains.

Why this blueprint rejected it: the Merkle chaining overhead is non-trivial: each segment seal requires a hash over all entries, adding O(segment_size) CPU cost per roll-over. More critically, the threat model for this blueprint (§ Threat Model) does not include a malicious operator; the adversaries are hardware failures, software bugs, and network partitions — not internal tampering. Adding Merkle proofs would increase segment roll-over latency (violating §4.1 p99.9 ≤ 50 ms under high roll frequency), complicate scavenge (deleting an event invalidates all subsequent segment hashes), and add implementation complexity with no benefit for the in-scope threat model. The CRC32C per-entry integrity check (Phase 2, ADR-09) addresses the actual threat (bit-rot, torn writes) without these costs.

Deferred Features Register

The following capabilities were identified during blueprint authoring as technically feasible but deferred beyond Phase 14 to keep the build sequence focused. Each entry names the phase where the deferral was first documented and the condition under which it would be promoted to an active phase.

# Deferred Feature Anchor Deferred In Promotion Condition
D1 Tiered storage (automatic segment offload to S3/GCS for cold data; read-back on demand) Phase 2 Phase 2 Total disk consumption exceeds 80% of cluster capacity; object-store client crate stabilises
D2 io_uring async I/O path (submission-queue batching for segment reads) Phase 2 Phase 2 tokio-uring reaches stable release; Linux kernel ≥ 5.10 becomes baseline
D3 WASM projection sandbox (run projection handlers in a WASM runtime for language-agnostic projections) Phase 8 Phase 8 Wasmtime Rust embedding API stabilises; handler hot-reload requirement emerges from users
D4 Multi-leader / active-active writes (allow writes to any node; use CRDT-based merge for conflict resolution) Phase 10 Phase 10 Strict linearisability requirement is relaxed by product; CRDT-compatible event schema is designed
D5 Schema registry integration (enforce event schema contracts via Confluent-compatible schema registry) Phase 13 Phase 13 Multi-team event bus use case emerges; cross-service event contracts need central governance
D6 Encryption at rest (AES-256-GCM segment encryption; key rotation without full re-encryption) Phase 2 Phase 2 Compliance requirement (SOC 2 Type II, HIPAA) is confirmed; key management service chosen
D7 Cross-cluster geo-replication (async replication of committed events to a geographically separate cluster) Phase 12 Phase 12 RPO > 0 for regional failure is accepted; WAN latency budget is defined
D8 Partition merge / split (dynamically change the number of partitions without downtime) Phase 12 Phase 12 Stream count grows beyond single-partition capacity; consistent-hash migration tooling is ready
D9 Built-in gRPC gateway (REST/HTTP transcoding for clients that cannot use gRPC) Phase 13 Phase 13 Browser client or legacy REST-only integration requirement emerges
D10 Reactive subscription backpressure protocol (client-driven credit-based flow control per gRPC stream) Phase 13 Phase 13 Slow-consumer disconnection rate exceeds SLO; credit-based backpressure is standardised in client SDK
D11 Cross-partition $all merge (globally totally-ordered $all view across partitions, not just per-partition position spaces) §1.9 Phase 12 External sequencer or hybrid-logical-clock merge design is adopted (HQ13, C12)
D12 OAuth/LDAP authentication provider (external identity-provider integration beyond the built-in token/mTLS auth hook) §1.9 Phase 13 Enterprise SSO/LDAP directory integration requirement emerges from an operator
D13 Backup streaming to S3-compatible object storage (continuous off-cluster backup of segments/snapshots) §1.9 Phase 14 Object-store client crate stabilises; RTO/RPO budget requires off-node backup beyond Raft replication
D14 Time-based event expiration (TTL) scavenge policy (age-based removal in addition to maxcount-based scavenge) §1.9 Phase 9 A product requirement for automatic data retention limits (not just operator-triggered soft-delete) emerges

Glossary

Alphabetical. Each term is implementation-relevant; references locked type/crate names where applicable.

Term Definition
$all stream The virtual stream comprising every event in the store in commit order; cursor is GlobalPosition.
Aggregate A domain object reconstituted by replaying a StreamId's events; state = fold(events) or fold(snapshot + tail).
Append-only log The fundamental storage primitive; records are written sequentially and never overwritten in place — see esdb-log.
At-least-once delivery Subscription/projection guarantee: an event is delivered at minimum once; idempotency + checkpoint converts this to exactly-once effect.
Catch-up subscription A subscription that first reads historical events from a stored Checkpoint position then transitions to live without gap; see esdb-subscribe.
Category The StreamId prefix before the first - delimiter (e.g. order for order-1234); used for category streams and projection filters.
Changefeed A live stream of committed events exposed to external consumers; implemented via catch-up + live subscription.
Checkpoint { subscription_or_projection, position: GlobalPosition } — the durable resume point ensuring a consumer can restart without re-reading from position 0.
Commit index Raft term: the highest log index known to be quorum-durable; entries at or below this index are committed and GlobalPosition-assigned.
Commit position The GlobalPosition assigned to an event when its Raft log entry reaches the commit index; the universal cursor for all readers.
Competing consumers Multiple subscriber instances in a persistent subscription group sharing delivery load; each event is sent to exactly one consumer.
Compaction / Scavenging Rewriting segments in esdb-storage to drop deleted, expired, or maxcount-exceeded events while preserving numbers/positions of live events.
CQRS Command-Query Responsibility Segregation; append path (command) is separate from read-model path (query); natural fit with event sourcing.
Event An immutable fact that has happened; represented as EventRecord; identified by EventId; versioned within its stream by EventNumber.
Event sourcing Persisting aggregate state as an ordered log of domain events; state is derived by replaying the log, never stored as a mutable snapshot row.
Event store A database optimised for event sourcing; core operations are append-with-version, read-stream, and subscribe-from-position.
EventRecord { event_id, stream, number, event_type, data: Bytes, metadata: Bytes, position, created } — the canonical persisted event; fields are immutable post-commit.
ExpectedVersion enum { Any, NoStream, StreamExists, Exact(EventNumber) } — the caller's declaration of the stream version it expects; enforces optimistic concurrency.
Fold / Replay Applying an ordered sequence of events to an initial state via a reducer function to produce aggregate state; state = fold(init, events).
fsync Kernel call flushing dirty pages to durable storage; called after every segment append in esdb-log to satisfy P8 / I4.
GlobalPosition u64 monotonic, unique commit offset in the global log; strictly increases; gap-free within a single-partition store; the merge key across partitions.
Idempotent handler A projection/subscription event handler whose repeated application of the same event yields the same read-model state; required for at-least-once delivery.
Leader election Raft mechanism: on heartbeat timeout a follower increments its term and requests votes; first node with quorum votes becomes leader.
Log matching Raft safety property: if two log entries share the same index and term, all preceding entries are identical; guarantees follower convergence.
Optimistic concurrency Concurrency strategy in which a writer declares its expected version; no pessimistic lock is held; a conflicting concurrent append is rejected by esdb-stream.
Partitioning / Sharding Distributing stream ownership across cluster nodes (or Raft groups) by hashing StreamId; handled in esdb-partition.
Persistent subscription A checkpointed subscription whose position survives restarts; supports competing consumers and ack/nack/park/retry.
Projection A continuously-maintained read model built by applying an idempotent handler to events from $all or a category; checkpointed by GlobalPosition.
Quorum (N/2)+1 nodes in an N-node Raft group; both elections and commit decisions require quorum acknowledgment.
Raft Distributed consensus algorithm (Ongaro & Ousterhout 2014) used in esdb-replication to replicate the global log and elect a leader.
Read model Derived queryable state produced by a projection; disposable and rebuildable from the log (P9).
Segment A fixed-size file in esdb-log holding a contiguous slice of log records; rolled over at a size threshold; the unit of scavenging.
Snapshot { stream, version: EventNumber, state: Bytes, position } — a fold checkpoint stored by esdb-snapshot; loading uses newest snapshot + tail replay.
Soft delete / Tombstone Marking a stream as deleted via a metadata event; the stream is inaccessible to normal reads; scavenging later removes the physical records.
Stream A named, ordered, append-only sequence of events belonging to one logical aggregate; identified by StreamId; versioned by EventNumber.
StreamId The logical stream / aggregate identity string; category is the prefix before -; hashed for partition routing.
Term Raft monotonic election counter; incremented on each new election; guards against stale leaders and split-brain.
Volatile subscription An in-memory subscription with no checkpoint; delivers only events appended after subscription creation; lost on subscriber restart.
WAL Write-Ahead Log semantics; in esdb-log, each append is persisted (fsynced) before the write is acknowledged, enabling crash recovery.
WrongExpectedVersion The error returned by esdb-stream when the current stream version does not match the caller's ExpectedVersion; write is rejected, nothing committed.

.github/workflows/ci.yml GitHub Actions CI: fmt, clippy, test, property tests, Raft safety suite.
.github/workflows/fuzz.yml Nightly cargo-fuzz run (libFuzzer targets).
.github/workflows/bench.yml Criterion benchmarks on main; publish results to gh-pages.
.github/workflows/conformance.yml Replay-conformance + Raft-safety harness gate.
.github/workflows/release.yml GitHub Actions release: build binaries, publish crate, tag.
esdb-core/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-core; no I/O dependencies.
esdb-core/src/lib.rs Re-exports all core types: StreamId, EventId, EventNumber, GlobalPosition, ExpectedVersion.
esdb-core/src/types.rs Type definitions and impls for core scalar types; category derivation logic.
esdb-core/src/error.rs WrongExpectedVersion, NoStream, and other base error variants.
esdb-core/src/serde.rs Serialization framing (length-prefix, CRC) for cross-crate wire/storage format.
esdb-core/tests/roundtrip.rs Property test: EventRecord serialization roundtrip (proptest).
esdb-event/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-event.
esdb-event/src/lib.rs Re-exports EventRecord, EventType, ContentType, metadata helpers.
esdb-event/src/record.rs EventRecord struct, builder, and field accessors.
esdb-event/src/versioning.rs Event-type schema versioning conventions and upcaster registry interface.
esdb-event/tests/metadata.rs Unit tests for metadata parsing and content-type negotiation.
esdb-log/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-log.
esdb-log/src/lib.rs Public API: Log, Segment, IndexEntry; re-exports.
esdb-log/src/segment.rs Fixed-size segment file: append, read, roll-over, CRC verification.
esdb-log/src/index.rs (stream, number)->offset and position->offset index structures; build + lookup.
esdb-log/src/recovery.rs Crash recovery: scan last segment, detect torn write, truncate, rebuild index.
esdb-log/src/global.rs Global ($all) log logic: monotonic GlobalPosition assignment, sequential commit ordering.
esdb-log/tests/crash_recovery.rs Fuzz + deterministic crash-recovery scenarios; torn-tail truncation assertions.
esdb-log/tests/index_rebuild.rs Full index rebuild from raw segment bytes; equivalence with incremental index.
esdb-stream/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-stream.
esdb-stream/src/lib.rs Public API: StreamEngine, append, read, tombstone.
esdb-stream/src/append.rs ExpectedVersion enforcement; gap-free EventNumber assignment; AppendResult.
esdb-stream/src/read.rs Forward/backward stream reads with paging cursor; consistent-read guarantees.
esdb-stream/src/idempotency.rs EventId dedup store; idempotent re-append detection.
esdb-stream/src/tombstone.rs Soft-delete: tombstone event write, stream-deleted checks, scavenge eligibility.
esdb-stream/tests/concurrency.rs Concurrent append races; WrongExpectedVersion linearizability assertions.
esdb-stream/tests/idempotency.rs Re-append same EventId → no duplicate; different EventId same position → error.
esdb-snapshot/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-snapshot.
esdb-snapshot/src/lib.rs Public API: SnapshotStore, write_snapshot, load_aggregate.
esdb-snapshot/src/store.rs Snapshot storage keyed by (stream, version); nearest-snapshot lookup.
esdb-snapshot/src/load.rs Aggregate load: newest snapshot + tail replay; schema-version invalidation.
esdb-snapshot/tests/fidelity.rs I9 invariant: snapshot + tail == full_replay property test.
esdb-projection/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-projection.
esdb-projection/src/lib.rs Public API: ProjectionEngine, register, rebuild, state.
esdb-projection/src/handler.rs Idempotent handler trait; apply + checkpoint transaction semantics.
esdb-projection/src/checkpoint.rs Durable checkpoint write/read for GlobalPosition; transactional with state update.
esdb-projection/src/rebuild.rs Full rebuild: reset checkpoint to 0, replay from global log start.
esdb-projection/tests/determinism.rs I8 invariant: incremental == full-replay state equivalence property test.
esdb-subscribe/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-subscribe.
esdb-subscribe/src/lib.rs Public API: SubscriptionManager, subscribe, ack, nack.
esdb-subscribe/src/catchup.rs Catch-up phase: read from checkpoint, transition to live without gap/duplicate.
esdb-subscribe/src/live.rs Live delivery path: event notification from esdb-log commit tail.
esdb-subscribe/src/persistent.rs Persistent subscription: checkpoint durability, competing consumer group state.
esdb-subscribe/src/backpressure.rs Flow control: bounded channel, slow-consumer detection, park on overflow.
esdb-subscribe/tests/gap_free.rs Gap/duplicate property test across catch-up→live boundary under concurrent appends.
esdb-replication/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-replication.
esdb-replication/src/lib.rs Public API: RaftNode, append_entry, apply, commit_index.
esdb-replication/src/leader.rs Leader append path: ReplicationEntry wrap, replicate to followers, advance commit on quorum ack.
esdb-replication/src/follower.rs Follower append path: log consistency check, apply entries, update match_index.
esdb-replication/src/election.rs Leader election: term increment, RequestVote RPC, randomised timeout, quorum vote count.
esdb-replication/src/membership.rs Cluster membership changes; joint-consensus or single-server add/remove.
esdb-replication/src/snapshot_transfer.rs Raft InstallSnapshot RPC for fast follower catch-up on large lag.
esdb-replication/tests/safety.rs Raft safety suite: linearizable appends under simulated partition, no committed-entry loss.
esdb-partition/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-partition.
esdb-partition/src/lib.rs Public API: PartitionRouter, route, rebalance.
esdb-partition/src/hash.rs StreamId consistent-hash to partition ID; deterministic, stable under node add.
esdb-partition/src/routing.rs Ownership table: partition → owning node; redirect non-owner requests to owner.
esdb-partition/src/rebalance.rs Partition transfer on membership change; stream-affinity and category-locality hints.
esdb-partition/tests/routing.rs Route correctness: every StreamId maps to exactly one owner; ownership after rebalance.
esdb-storage/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-storage.
esdb-storage/src/lib.rs Public API: StorageEngine, ScavengePlan, execute_scavenge.
esdb-storage/src/format.rs On-disk layout: magic bytes, header, record framing, index file format.
esdb-storage/src/scavenge.rs Scavenge planner: compute ScavengePlan (segment, live-ranges); executor rewrites segments.
esdb-storage/src/verify.rs Checksum verification pass over all segments; reports corrupt records.
esdb-storage/tests/scavenge_safety.rs I10 invariant: live events preserved, dead events absent, numbers/positions unchanged.
esdb-server/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-server.
esdb-server/src/lib.rs Server entry-point wiring; initialises all sub-engines and gRPC/HTTP listeners.
esdb-server/src/grpc/append.rs gRPC Append handler: validate, route, call esdb-stream, return AppendResult.
esdb-server/src/grpc/read.rs gRPC Read and ReadAll handlers; streaming responses; cursor/paging.
esdb-server/src/grpc/subscribe.rs gRPC Subscribe streaming handler; backpressure bridging to esdb-subscribe.
esdb-server/src/grpc/project.rs gRPC projection management: register, query state, trigger rebuild.
esdb-server/src/http/health.rs HTTP /health and /metrics endpoints; Prometheus gauge for lag, commit index, segment count.
esdb-server/src/auth.rs Authentication middleware: token validation, per-stream ACLs.
esdb-server/src/gossip.rs Cluster gossip: node membership heartbeat, leader advertisement, health state.
esdb-server/src/config.rs Server configuration struct: listen addresses, data dir, cluster peers, auth settings.
esdb-server/proto/eventstore.proto Protobuf definitions for all gRPC service methods and message types.
esdb-server/tests/integration.rs End-to-end integration: multi-node cluster, append, read, subscribe, projection, failover.
esdb-cli/Cargo.toml Crate manifest for esdb-cli.
esdb-cli/src/main.rs CLI entry-point; dispatches to subcommand modules.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/append.rs esdb append subcommand: read events from stdin or file, call gRPC Append.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/read.rs esdb read subcommand: read stream or $all, print JSON events to stdout.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/subscribe.rs esdb subscribe subcommand: stream live events to stdout; Ctrl-C clean shutdown.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/project.rs esdb project subcommand: register, query, rebuild projections.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/cluster.rs esdb cluster subcommand: show node roles, commit index, leader, membership.
esdb-cli/src/cmd/scavenge.rs esdb scavenge subcommand: trigger online scavenge, print plan and progress.
esdb-cli/src/client.rs gRPC client wrapper used by all subcommands; TLS + auth configuration.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
Phase 1 esdb-core + esdb-event: all core types, EventRecord, serialization framing 1 week Schema framing divergence across crates; UUID collision Property roundtrip passes; EventRecord serde deterministic; no I/O in crate
Phase 2 esdb-log: segment files, CRC, fsync, index, crash recovery 2 weeks Torn-write scenarios missed; index divergence after crash Recovery fuzz passes; sequential + random read correct; segment roll-over verified
Phase 3 esdb-stream: append with ExpectedVersion, gap-free numbering, idempotent re-append, tombstone 2 weeks Race between concurrent appenders; EventId dedup false-positive Concurrency test: WrongExpectedVersion linearizable; idempotency test passes
Phase 4 Global ($all) log, GlobalPosition assignment, $all read in commit order 1 week Position gaps under concurrent multi-stream appends Every commit gets unique monotonic position; $all read returns events in commit order
Phase 5 esdb-stream / query: read forward/backward, $all, category, event-type filters, paging 1 week Consistent-read semantics under in-flight appends; paging boundary edge cases All filter combinations covered; is_end correct at stream tail; paging cursor deterministic
Phase 6 esdb-snapshot: write, load (snap + tail), schema-version invalidation 1 week Snapshot written at wrong version; tail replay ordering I9 property test passes: snapshot + tail == full_replay
Phase 7 esdb-subscribe: catch-up + live, persistent + volatile, backpressure, filter 2 weeks Gap or duplicate at catch-up→live boundary; slow-consumer blocking fast path Gap-free property test passes under concurrent appends; slow-consumer does not stall others
Phase 8 esdb-projection: idempotent handlers, checkpointed read models, rebuild 1.5 weeks Non-idempotent handler corrupts read model on retry; checkpoint lag causes stale state I8 determinism test passes; rebuild produces identical state to incremental; checkpoint survives crash
Phase 9 esdb-storage: crash recovery hardening, checksum verification, online scavenge 2 weeks Scavenge rewrite races with concurrent reads; checksum mismatch on valid records I10 scavenge-safety test passes; online scavenge completes without blocking reads
Phase 10 esdb-replication: Raft global log replication, quorum commit, follower apply 3 weeks Log divergence between leader and follower; match_index accounting error Raft safety suite passes; no committed entry lost under 1-node failure in 3-node cluster
Phase 11 Leader election, membership, failover, client redirect 2 weeks Split-brain on asymmetric partition; stale-leader writes after isolation Election test: single leader per term; client redirected to leader within timeout
Phase 12 esdb-partition: StreamId hash routing, ownership table, rebalancing, cross-partition $all 2 weeks Ownership race during rebalance; $all merge out-of-order across partitions Routing correctness test; category affinity preserved; $all merge order monotonic
Phase 13 esdb-server gRPC/HTTP, competing consumers, ack/nack/park/retry, auth, gossip 2 weeks Backpressure propagation across gRPC stream; competing-consumer delivery duplicate under nack Integration test: multi-node append/subscribe/project end-to-end; competing consumers each-once delivery
Phase 14 Hardening: replay conformance, Raft partition fuzz, crash-recovery fuzz, throughput bench, backup/export 2 weeks Performance regression under fsync; fuzz exposes edge case in torn-write recovery All conformance and fuzz suites green; p99 append ≤ target; throughput bench baseline recorded
Full Build All 14 phases complete; cluster integration test green on 3-node and 5-node; scavenge + backup exercised 26 weeks total Scope creep in partitioning; Raft edge cases delaying Phase 10–11 All workspace tests pass; CI green; ops runbook reviewed; CHANGELOG entry for v1.0.0
